# exam-listening-set-1: Listening for review

Transcript, questions, keys, evidence, explanations (en, ru, uk), what the solvers answered and what the transcriber heard. Audio: `audio/<test>-p<N>.mp3`.

### Questions

**form_completion** — Complete the form below. Write ONE WORD AND/OR A NUMBER for each answer. (Kingsway Van Hire – Booking Form)

- **1** Customer name: Megan ______ → **Holroyd** (solvers Holroyd / Holroyd; controller Holroyd)
  - evidence: "it's Megan Holroyd. Holroyd is H-O-L-R-O-Y-D."
  - en: The caller gives her name and spells her surname letter by letter: H-O-L-R-O-Y-D.
  - ru: Звонящая называет своё имя и диктует фамилию по буквам: "H-O-L-R-O-Y-D".
  - uk: Абонентка називає своє ім'я і диктує прізвище по літерах: "H-O-L-R-O-Y-D".
- **2** Size of van: ______ → **medium** (solvers medium / medium; controller medium)
  - evidence: "I'll take your advice and go for the medium one, then."
  - en: She first thought about the small van, but the assistant says her sofa and bed would not fit, so she chooses the medium one.
  - ru: Сначала она думала о маленьком фургоне ("small van"), но сотрудник говорит, что её диван и кровать туда не поместятся, поэтому она выбирает средний — "medium".
  - uk: Спочатку вона думала про маленький фургон ("small van"), але співробітник каже, що її диван і ліжко туди не помістяться, тож вона обирає середній — "medium".
- **3** Collection day: ______ → **Saturday** (solvers Saturday / Saturday; controller Saturday)
  - evidence: "so it'll have to be Saturday morning"
  - en: She wanted Friday evening, but her friend cannot get off work, so she will collect the van on Saturday.
  - ru: Она хотела в пятницу вечером ("Friday evening"), но её друг не может отпроситься с работы, поэтому она заберёт фургон в субботу — "Saturday".
  - uk: Вона хотіла в п'ятницю ввечері ("Friday evening"), але її друг не може відпроситися з роботи, тож вона забере фургон у суботу — "Saturday".
- **4** Collection time: ______ a.m. → **8.30 | 8:30** (solvers 8.30 / 8.30; controller 8.30)
  - evidence: "So the earliest you can collect it is 8.30."
  - en: The office opens at eight, but the vans must be checked and cleaned first, so she can collect it at 8.30, not eight.
  - ru: Офис открывается в восемь, но фургоны сначала нужно проверить и почистить, поэтому забрать его она сможет в "8.30", а не в "eight".
  - uk: Офіс відчиняється о восьмій, але фургони спершу треба перевірити й почистити, тож забрати його вона зможе о "8.30", а не о "eight".
- **5** New address: Flat 4, 27 ______ Road → **Lindley** (solvers Lindley / Lindley; controller Lindley)
  - evidence: "It's Flat 4, 27 Lindley Road. That's L-I-N-D-L-E-Y."
  - en: The caller gives her new address and spells the street name: L-I-N-D-L-E-Y.
  - ru: Звонящая называет свой новый адрес и диктует название улицы по буквам: "L-I-N-D-L-E-Y".
  - uk: Абонентка називає свою нову адресу і диктує назву вулиці по літерах: "L-I-N-D-L-E-Y".
- **6** Mobile number: 07700 900 ______ → **362** (solvers 362 / 362; controller 362)
  - evidence: "So that's 07700 900 362."
  - en: She first says 326 by mistake, then corrects it to 362, and the assistant repeats the number to check it.
  - ru: Сначала она по ошибке говорит "326", затем исправляется на "362", и сотрудник повторяет номер, чтобы его проверить.
  - uk: Спочатку вона помилково каже "326", потім виправляє на "362", і співробітник повторює номер, щоб його перевірити.

**note_completion** — Complete the notes below. Write ONE WORD AND/OR A NUMBER for each answer. (Extras and collection)

- **7** Extra booked: furniture ______ at £5 → **blankets** (solvers blankets / blankets; controller blankets)
  - evidence: "But the blankets would be useful."
  - en: She does not need a trolley because she has borrowed one, but she wants the blankets so she does not scratch anything.

  - uk: Візок ("trolley") їй не потрібен, бо вона вже позичила його, а от ковдри ("blankets") вона хоче взяти, щоб нічого не подряпати.
- **8** Deposit: £______ (returned if there is no damage) → **150** (solvers 150 / 150; controller 150)
  - evidence: "It used to be £200, but it's now £150."
  - en: The deposit was £200 in the past, but now it is £150.
  - ru: Раньше залог составлял "£200", но теперь он "£150".
  - uk: Раніше застава становила "£200", а тепер — "£150".
- **9** Proof of address: a recent ______, not a bank statement → **bill** (solvers bill / bill; controller bill)
  - evidence: "It needs to be a recent bill, like gas or electricity."
  - en: The assistant says a bank statement is not enough; she must bring a recent bill, such as a gas or electricity one.
  - ru: Сотрудник говорит, что банковской выписки ("bank statement") недостаточно: нужно принести недавний счёт ("bill"), например за газ или электричество ("gas", "electricity").
  - uk: Співробітник каже, що банківської виписки ("bank statement") недостатньо: треба принести нещодавній рахунок ("bill"), наприклад за газ чи електрику ("gas", "electricity").
- **10** Allow ______ minutes at the office when collecting → **twenty | 20** (solvers 20 / twenty; controller twenty)
  - evidence: "you should allow about twenty minutes"
  - en: Most people expect ten minutes, but because of the checks and photos, the assistant says to allow twenty minutes.
  - ru: Большинство рассчитывает на "ten minutes", но из-за проверок и фотографий сотрудник советует закладывать "twenty minutes".
  - uk: Більшість розраховує на "ten minutes", але через перевірки й фотографії співробітник радить закладати "twenty minutes".

## l3|p2: succeeded

**Welcome to Riverbend Botanic Gardens.** You will hear a volunteer guide welcoming a group of visitors to a botanic garden.

Speakers: Aroha Tremain (female, new_zealand, volunteer guide at a botanic garden, elevenlabs 7ceZgj78jCCeAW93ItNk)

### Transcript

0. [0:40] **Aroha Tremain:** Good morning, everyone, and welcome to the Riverbend Botanic Gardens. My name's Aroha Tremain, and I'm one of the volunteer guides here. Before you head off on your own, I'll quickly explain where everything is, and then I'll tell you what's on this month. You've each got a map, so have a look at that as I go.
1. [0:59] **Aroha Tremain:** Right. We're standing at the main gate, at the bottom of your map. The main path runs straight north from here, right up through the middle of the gardens. As you walk up it, the first building on your right is the Visitor Centre. That's where you'll find information and our gift shop.
2. [1:15] **Aroha Tremain:** Now, a lot of people assume the café's in the Visitor Centre too, but it isn't. It's directly across the path from it, on your left as you walk north, right next to the Rose Garden. They do a lovely flat white, if you need one.
   - heard (WER 0.022): Now, a lot of people assume the cafe's in the visitor centre too, but it isn't. It's directly across the path from it, on your left as you walk north, right next to the rose garden. They do a lovely flat white if you need one.
3. [1:29] **Aroha Tremain:** Keep going and you'll reach a crossroads, where a second path runs from east to west. Now, the toilets. If you turn right at the crossroads and walk all the way to the end of that path, they're on your right-hand side. They're the only toilets in the gardens, by the way, so it's worth remembering where they are.
4. [1:48] **Aroha Tremain:** On the other side of the path, facing the toilets, is the herb garden. It's a favourite with keen cooks, and you're welcome to touch and smell the plants. Just please don't pick them!
5. [1:59] **Aroha Tremain:** Okay, back to the crossroads. If you carry straight on north, the building immediately on your left is the Fernery. It's cool and shady in there, so it's a nice escape on a warm day. The building opposite it, on the right, is the staff office, and I'm afraid that's not open to visitors.
   - heard (WER 0.019): OK, back to the crossroads. If you carry straight on north, the building immediately on your left is the fernery. It's cool and shady in there, so it's a nice escape on a warm day. The building opposite it, on the right, is the staff office, and I'm afraid that's not open to visitors.
6. [2:18] **Aroha Tremain:** If you've got children with you, they'll want the children's garden. For that, go to the crossroads and turn left, so you're heading west. Walk to the very end of the path, and it's on your right, just south of the Duck Pond. There's a sandpit and a little hedge maze in there.
7. [2:35] **Aroha Tremain:** Last one. The plant shop sells seedlings grown in our own nursery, and it's well worth a visit. Go right to the top of the main path, where you'll see the big Glasshouse. The shop's in the small building just to the east of it, so that's on your right as you face the Glasshouse.
8. [3:25] **Aroha Tremain:** Right, so that's where everything is. Now, what's on this month. We run free guided walks every day. They used to leave at 10:30 am, but this month they start at 11 am, from just outside the Visitor Centre. Do try to get there ten minutes early, though, because they fill up fast.
9. [3:44] **Aroha Tremain:** In the Glasshouse, we'd planned to have our orchid display this month, but the orchids are flowering late this year, so that's been moved to January. Instead, there's a special exhibition of carnivorous plants, the ones that trap insects. The cactus collection's in there all year round, of course.
10. [4:02] **Aroha Tremain:** A few people have asked about the Rose Garden. It's open as usual, but don't expect too much just yet. Most of the roses are still in bud, and they won't be at their best until about mid-December.
11. [4:14] **Aroha Tremain:** Now, this Saturday there's a gardening workshop. Lots of people ask us about pruning, but we do that one in winter. This one's all about growing vegetables from seed, and everyone takes home a tray of seedlings. Next month's workshop is on composting, if that's more your thing.
12. [4:31] **Aroha Tremain:** It's $15 per person, and there are only twenty places. Please don't try to book on our website, because the booking form isn't working this week. We can't take bookings over the phone, either. Just pop into the Visitor Centre and put your name down at the desk.
   - voice got: It's fifteen dollars per person, and there are only twenty places. Please don't try to book on our website, because the booking form isn't working this week. We can't take bookings over the phone, either. Just pop into the Visitor Centre and put your name down at the desk.
13. [4:46] **Aroha Tremain:** And finally, on Sunday, sorry, Friday evening, there's a twilight concert on the lawn by the Duck Pond. You don't need to bring chairs, we've got plenty, and the paths will be lit, so leave your torch at home. But it does get chilly once the sun goes down, so bring an extra layer.
14. [5:05] **Aroha Tremain:** Okay, that's all from me. Enjoy your visit, and if you've got any questions, come and find me. I'll be by the café.

- **11** Café → **A** (solvers A / A; controller A)
  - evidence: "It's directly across the path from it, on your left as you walk north, right next to the Rose Garden."
  - en: The café is not inside the Visitor Centre. It is opposite it, on the left as you walk north from the gate, next to the Rose Garden.
  - ru: Кафе находится не внутри "Visitor Centre", а напротив него: слева, если идти от ворот на север, рядом с "Rose Garden".
  - uk: Кафе розташоване не всередині "Visitor Centre", а навпроти нього: ліворуч, якщо йти від воріт на північ, поруч із "Rose Garden".
- **12** Toilets → **B** (solvers B / B; controller B)
  - evidence: "turn right at the crossroads and walk all the way to the end of that path, they're on your right-hand side"
  - en: Turning right at the crossroads means walking east. At the end of the path, the right-hand side is the south side, so the toilets are B, not the building on the north side.
  - ru: Повернуть направо на перекрёстке значит идти на восток. В конце дорожки правая сторона — южная, поэтому туалеты — это B, а не здание на северной стороне.
  - uk: Повернути праворуч на перехресті означає йти на схід. Наприкінці доріжки права сторона — південна, тож туалети — це B, а не будівля на північному боці.
- **13** Herb garden → **F** (solvers F / F; controller F)
  - evidence: "On the other side of the path, facing the toilets, is the herb garden."
  - en: The herb garden is opposite the toilets, on the north side of the east end of the cross path.
  - ru: Сад пряных трав находится напротив туалетов, на северной стороне восточного конца поперечной дорожки.
  - uk: Сад пряних трав розташований навпроти туалетів, на північному боці східного кінця поперечної доріжки.
- **14** Fernery → **C** (solvers C / C; controller C)
  - evidence: "If you carry straight on north, the building immediately on your left is the Fernery."
  - en: Going straight on north from the crossroads, the left side is west, so the Fernery is C. The building on the right is the staff office.
  - ru: Если от перекрёстка идти прямо на север, слева будет запад, поэтому "Fernery" — это C. Здание справа — служебный офис персонала.
  - uk: Якщо від перехрестя йти прямо на північ, ліворуч буде захід, тому "Fernery" — це C. Будівля праворуч — службовий офіс персоналу.
- **15** Children's garden → **D** (solvers D / D; controller D)
  - evidence: "Walk to the very end of the path, and it's on your right, just south of the Duck Pond."
  - en: Turning left at the crossroads means walking west. At the very end, on the right (north) side and just below the Duck Pond, is the children's garden. You pass the Fernery on the way, but that is not at the end.
  - ru: Повернуть налево на перекрёстке значит идти на запад. В самом конце, справа (с северной стороны), прямо под "Duck Pond", находится детский сад-парк для детей. По пути вы проходите мимо "Fernery", но это ещё не конец дорожки.
  - uk: Повернути ліворуч на перехресті означає йти на захід. У самому кінці, праворуч (з північного боку), одразу під "Duck Pond", розташований сад для дітей. Дорогою ви проходите повз "Fernery", але це ще не кінець доріжки.

**How honeybees share the location of food.** You will hear a university lecturer giving a talk about how honeybees communicate where food can be found.

Speakers: Nathan Kowalski (male, north_american, university lecturer in animal behaviour, xai sal)

### Transcript

0. [0:42] **Nathan Kowalski:** Good morning, everyone. Today I want to look at a question that puzzled people for a very long time: how does a honeybee that's found food tell the rest of the colony where it is? The answer, or most of it anyway, is a behavior called the waggle dance. And at the end we'll look at the limits of that idea, because it's not quite as neat as the textbooks suggest.
1. [1:02] **Nathan Kowalski:** Let's start with Karl von Frisch, the Austrian biologist who did the classic work on this. He wanted to control exactly where the bees found food, and real flowers are very hard to control. So instead he trained bees to visit a small dish of sugar water, which he could move around the countryside wherever he liked.
2. [1:20] **Nathan Kowalski:** Of course, he needed to know which bee was which. He considered clipping a tiny piece off their wings, but that would've changed the way they flew. So he marked each visitor with a little dot of paint, using different colors in a kind of code.
   - heard (WER 0.065): Of course, he needed to know which bee was which. He considered clipping a tiny piece off their wings, but that would have changed the way they flew, so he marked each visitor with a little dot of paint, using different colors and a kind of code.
3. [1:33] **Nathan Kowalski:** Then he watched what those bees did back home. A normal hive is dark and closed, so he used what's called an observation hive. That's basically a frame of comb with glass sides, so you can see what's going on inside without disturbing anyone.
4. [1:47] **Nathan Kowalski:** What he saw were two different dances. If the food was close to the hive, within about fifty meters, the returning bee ran around in small circles, first one way, then the other. He called this the round dance. It basically says, there's food nearby, go and look, without giving much detail.
5. [2:05] **Nathan Kowalski:** For food farther away, the bee performs the waggle dance. She runs in a straight line, shaking her body from side to side, then loops back around and repeats it. Now, the direction of that straight run is the clever part.
6. [2:18] **Nathan Kowalski:** On the vertical comb, straight up stands for the direction of the sun. So if the bee waggles at, say, forty degrees to the right of vertical, the food is forty degrees to the right of the sun. Not magnetic north, not the hive entrance. The sun.
7. [2:32] **Nathan Kowalski:** What about distance? A lot of students assume it's shown by how fast the bee shakes. Actually, the speed of the waggling hardly changes. It's the duration of the waggle run, how long it lasts, that tells the others how far to fly. So a longer run simply means a longer flight.
8. [2:48] **Nathan Kowalski:** As a rough guide, one second of waggling means the food's about 750 meters away. You'll see some books say a kilometer, but it varies between colonies, so treat it as approximate. Food two kilometers off might get a waggle run of nearly three seconds.
9. [3:03] **Nathan Kowalski:** Now, remember it's pitch black inside a hive, so the other bees can't actually see any of this. Instead, they follow close behind the dancer, pick up the vibrations, and keep touching her body with their antennae. They also pick up the scent of the flowers she's been visiting, which helps them later on.
   - heard (WER 0.019): Now, remember it's pitch black inside a hive, so the other bees can't actually see any of this. Instead, they follow close behind the dancer, pick up the vibrations, and keep touching her body with their antennae. They also pick up the scent of the flower she's been visiting, which helps them later on.
10. [3:20] **Nathan Kowalski:** Right. So how do we know the dance really works like this? Von Frisch's evidence came from watching which feeding sites new bees turned up at. A stronger test came in the late 1980s, sorry, the early 1990s, when a team in Denmark built a robot bee. It was about the size of a real one, made of brass and driven by a computer. When it danced in the hive, real bees flew off to the spot its dance pointed to.
11. [3:44] **Nathan Kowalski:** Another test used radar. Researchers fixed tiny transponders to bees that had just followed a dance, then tracked their flights across open fields. Most flew off in roughly the direction the dance had shown, although many still had to search around once they got there.
12. [3:59] **Nathan Kowalski:** There's also the question of how a bee measures distance in the first place. Researchers in Australia trained bees to fly down a narrow tunnel with patterned walls to reach food. Afterwards, the bees danced as if they'd flown many times farther than they really had. So they judge distance from how fast the world moves past their eyes, not from how tired they get.
13. [4:20] **Nathan Kowalski:** Okay, finally, the limits. The dance is impressive, but it's not the whole story. When researchers watched hives closely, they found that a lot of experienced foragers ignore dances most of the time. They'd rather go back to places they know, relying on their memory of sites that worked before, along with smell.
   - heard (WER 0.019): Okay, finally, the limits. The dance is impressive, but it's not the whole story. When researchers watched hives closely, they found that a lot of experienced foragers ignored dances most of the time. They'd rather go back to places they know, relying on their memory of sites that worked before, along with smell.
14. [4:37] **Nathan Kowalski:** Other experiments deliberately scrambled the dance, by laying the comb flat and keeping the hive dark, so the direction information was lost. In many temperate habitats, those colonies collected about as much food as normal ones. The dance only made a clear difference where food comes in rare, rich patches, as in tropical forest.
15. [4:56] **Nathan Kowalski:** So the waggle dance is real, and it's remarkable. But it's one tool among several, and how much it matters depends on where the bees live. Next week, we'll see how ants solve the same problem using chemical trails.

### Questions

**note_completion** — Complete the notes below. Write ONE WORD ONLY for each answer. (The honeybee waggle dance)

- **31** Von Frisch's method: bees trained to feed from a ______ of sugar water instead of real flowers → **dish** (solvers dish / dish; controller dish)
  - evidence: "he trained bees to visit a small dish of sugar water"
  - en: The lecturer says real flowers were hard to control, so von Frisch used a small dish of sugar water that he could move around.
  - ru: Лектор говорит, что с настоящими цветами было трудно работать, поэтому von Frisch использовал небольшую тарелочку с "sugar water" (сахарной водой), которую можно было переставлять с места на место.
  - uk: Лектор каже, що зі справжніми квітами було важко працювати, тож von Frisch використовував невелику мисочку з "sugar water" (цукровою водою), яку міг переставляти з місця на місце.
- **32** Each bee identified by a mark of ______ in a color code → **paint** (solvers paint / paint; controller paint)
  - evidence: "he marked each visitor with a little dot of paint"
  - en: He thought about clipping the wings but rejected the idea because it would change how the bees flew. He used dots of paint instead.
  - ru: Он думал о том, чтобы подрезать пчёлам крылья, но отказался от этой идеи: это изменило бы то, как они летают. Вместо этого он ставил на них точки краской ("paint").
  - uk: Він думав про те, щоб підрізати бджолам крила, але відмовився від цієї ідеї: це змінило б те, як вони літають. Натомість він ставив на них цятки фарбою ("paint").
- **33** Dances watched in an observation hive with ______ sides → **glass** (solvers glass / glass; controller glass)
  - evidence: "a frame of comb with glass sides"
  - en: An observation hive is a frame of comb with glass sides, so people can see inside without disturbing the bees.
  - ru: "Observation hive" — это рамка с сотами и стеклянными стенками ("glass"), поэтому можно заглянуть внутрь, не беспокоя пчёл.
  - uk: "Observation hive" — це рамка із сотами та скляними стінками ("glass"), тож можна зазирнути всередину, не турбуючи бджіл.
- **34** Round dance: means the food is ______ to the hive → **close** (solvers close / close; controller close)
  - evidence: "If the food was close to the hive, within about fifty meters"
  - en: The round dance is used when food is close to the hive, within about fifty meters. The waggle dance is used for food farther away.
  - ru: "Round dance" используется, когда корм находится близко к улью — примерно в пределах "fifty meters". "Waggle dance" — когда корм дальше.
  - uk: "Round dance" використовується, коли корм розташований близько до вулика — приблизно в межах "fifty meters". "Waggle dance" — коли корм далі.
- **35** Waggle dance: angle of the straight run from vertical shows direction in relation to the ______ → **sun** (solvers sun / sun; controller sun)
  - evidence: "straight up stands for the direction of the sun"
  - en: Straight up on the comb stands for the sun's direction. The lecturer says it is not magnetic north or the hive entrance.
  - ru: Направление строго вверх по сотам обозначает направление на солнце ("sun"). Лектор уточняет, что это не магнитный север и не вход в улей.
  - uk: Напрямок точно вгору по сотах позначає напрямок на сонце ("sun"). Лектор уточнює, що це не магнітна північ і не вхід до вулика.
- **36** Distance is shown by the ______ of the waggle run → **duration** (solvers duration / duration; controller duration)
  - evidence: "It's the duration of the waggle run, how long it lasts"
  - en: Many students think the speed of shaking shows distance, but the lecturer says speed hardly changes. It is the duration, or how long the run lasts.
  - ru: Многие студенты думают, что расстояние показывает скорость покачиваний, но лектор говорит, что скорость почти не меняется. Важна "duration" — то есть сколько длится этот пробег.
  - uk: Багато студентів думають, що відстань показує швидкість похитувань, але лектор каже, що швидкість майже не змінюється. Важить "duration" — тобто скільки триває цей пробіг.
- **37** One second of waggling ≈ food about ______ meters away → **750** (solvers 750 / 750; controller 750)
  - evidence: "one second of waggling means the food's about 750 meters away"
  - en: One second means about 750 meters. A kilometer is what some books say, and two kilometers is a separate example.
  - ru: Одна секунда соответствует примерно "750" метрам. Километр — это то, что пишут в некоторых книгах, а два километра — отдельный пример.
  - uk: Одна секунда відповідає приблизно "750" метрам. Кілометр — це те, що пишуть у деяких книжках, а два кілометри — окремий приклад.
- **38** Hive is dark, so followers stay in contact with the dancer using their ______ → **antennae** (solvers antennae / antennae; controller antennae)
  - evidence: "keep touching her body with their antennae"
  - en: The other bees can't see the dance in the dark, so they feel vibrations and keep touching the dancer with their antennae.
  - ru: В темноте другие пчёлы не видят танца, поэтому они ощущают вибрации ("vibrations") и постоянно касаются танцующей пчелы усиками ("antennae").
  - uk: У темряві інші бджоли не бачать танцю, тому вони відчувають вібрації ("vibrations") і постійно торкаються бджоли, що танцює, вусиками ("antennae").
- **39** Danish team: a computer-driven ______ bee sent real bees to a chosen spot → **robot** (solvers robot / robot; controller robot)
  - evidence: "a team in Denmark built a robot bee"
  - en: In the early 1990s, a team in Denmark built a robot bee made of brass and driven by a computer. Real bees flew where its dance pointed.
  - ru: В начале 1990-х команда в Дании ("Denmark") построила пчелу-робота из латуни ("brass"), которой управлял компьютер. Настоящие пчёлы летели туда, куда указывал её танец.
  - uk: На початку 1990-х команда в Данії ("Denmark") створила бджолу-робота з латуні ("brass"), якою керував комп'ютер. Справжні бджоли летіли туди, куди вказував її танець.
- **40** Flights of bees that had followed a dance were tracked using ______ → **radar** (solvers radar / radar; controller radar)
  - evidence: "Another test used radar."
  - en: Researchers put tiny transponders on bees and tracked them by radar. Most flew in roughly the direction the dance showed.
  - ru: Исследователи прикрепляли к пчёлам крошечные транспондеры ("transponders") и отслеживали их с помощью радара ("radar"). Большинство пчёл летели примерно в том направлении, которое показывал танец.
  - uk: Дослідники прикріплювали до бджіл крихітні транспондери ("transponders") і відстежували їх за допомогою радара ("radar"). Більшість бджіл летіли приблизно в тому напрямку, який показував танець.

## l4|p2: succeeded

**Briefing for festival staff.** You will hear a manager talking to a group of new staff on the day before a summer music festival opens.

Speakers: Claire Hollis (female, british, festival staff manager, elevenlabs FF59babHL8N8gfTgtBMT)

### Transcript

0. [0:42] **Claire Hollis:** Good morning, everyone, and welcome to the Larkmoor Music Festival. My name's Claire Hollis, and I'm the staff manager, so for the next four days I'm the person you'll see running around with a clipboard. Before we hand out uniforms, I want to go through a few things: the jobs, your shifts, the rules, and who to contact when something goes wrong. It shouldn't take more than a quarter of an hour.
1. [1:05] **Claire Hollis:** Right, jobs first. Most of you applied for a particular role, and we've tried to give everyone what they asked for. Bar work, for example, is completely full now, and so is ticket scanning at the entrance gates, so we won't be moving anyone into those teams.
2. [1:21] **Claire Hollis:** Where we're really short is in the car parks, directing the traffic, and in the recycling team, picking up litter. Most visitors arrive on Thursday afternoon, sorry, Friday afternoon, so that's when the car parks are busiest. Litter picking isn't glamorous, I know, but it's often the job people enjoy most by Sunday. The information tents, on the other hand, have more than enough volunteers already.
   - heard (WER 0.045): Where we're really short is in the car parks, directing the traffic, and in the recycling team, picking up litter. Most visitors arrive on Friday afternoon, so that's when the car parks are busiest. Litter picking isn't glamorous, I know, but it's often the job people enjoy most by Sunday. The information tents, on the other hand, have more than enough volunteers already.
3. [1:47] **Claire Hollis:** Okay, shifts. Each one is six hours, not eight, as it said in some of the old adverts. You need to sign in at the crew check-in tent at least fifteen minutes before you start. Every shift includes a thirty-minute break, and before anyone asks, yes, you're paid for it.
4. [2:05] **Claire Hollis:** A few of you have asked about night shifts. There's no extra pay for those, I'm afraid, but you do get the following day off. And the rota is drawn up by my team, so you can't pick your own shifts online, although you can ask to change them, which I'll come back to.
5. [2:21] **Claire Hollis:** Now, rules. Some of them might surprise you. Phones, for instance: we'd actually like you to keep them switched on, because we send updates to a staff group chat. And photos are fine anywhere, even backstage, as long as you don't post the backstage ones online.
6. [2:37] **Claire Hollis:** But there are two rules we're very strict about. You mustn't drink alcohol while you're wearing your staff T-shirt, even after your shift if you haven't changed. And your wristband is for you alone, so no bringing friends or family onto the site, not even for half an hour. Oh, and footwear: trainers are fine, you don't need safety boots, just nothing open-toed.
7. [3:33] **Claire Hollis:** Right, the last thing, and probably the most important, is who to contact. You'll get a card with all the numbers on, but let me go through it. If you need to change a shift, say you've swapped with a friend, you might think your area supervisor can sort it out, but they can't. That has to go through the staff office, which is the green cabin behind the main stage.
8. [3:56] **Claire Hollis:** If your wristband gets damaged, or you lose it, go to the accreditation desk at the staff entrance. They'll print you a new one, but it costs £15, so do look after it.
   - voice got: If your wristband gets damaged, or you lose it, go to the accreditation desk at the staff entrance. They'll print you a new one, but it costs fifteen pounds, so do look after it.
9. [4:07] **Claire Hollis:** Next, unattended bags. Please don't pick them up and carry them over to lost property, and don't open them. Just radio security control and stay nearby until someone arrives.
10. [4:19] **Claire Hollis:** Something that happens every year is a child who's lost their parents. **Glaciers as stores of fresh water.** You will hear a university lecturer in earth sciences giving a talk about glaciers and the fresh water they hold.

Speakers: Hamish Calloway (male, new_zealand, university lecturer in earth sciences, elevenlabs 1HGaTm3UFxAyxae5H0GU)

### Transcript

0. [0:43] **Hamish Calloway:** Good morning, everyone. I'm Hamish Calloway, and today we're continuing our unit on the water cycle by looking at glaciers, which, as you'll see, are enormous stores of fresh water. I'll start with how they form, then look at how they release water to rivers and farms, and I'll finish with what happens when they shrink.
   - heard (WER 0.018): Good morning, everyone. I'm Hamish Callaway, and today we're continuing our unit on the water cycle by looking at glaciers, which, as you'll see, are enormous stores of fresh water. I'll start with how they form, then look at how they release water to rivers and farms, and I'll finish with what happens when they shrink.
1. [1:02] **Hamish Calloway:** Right, so how does a glacier form? Lots of places get heavy snow in winter, but that alone isn't enough. For a glacier to develop, the snow has to survive the summer, so that some of it's still lying there when the next winter's snow arrives. Then it builds up, layer on layer, year after year.
   - heard (WER 0.036): Right, so how does a glacier form? Lots of places get heavy snow in winter, but that alone isn’t enough. For a glacier to develop, the snow has to survive the summer, so that some of it is still lying there when the next winter’s snow arrives. Then it builds up, layer on layer, year after year.
2. [1:23] **Hamish Calloway:** As those layers get thicker, something interesting happens underneath. The weight of the new snow presses down on the older snow, and the grains get packed closer and closer together. Over time the air between the grains is squeezed out, and what you end up with is dense, solid ice. In very cold places, that can take well over a century.
3. [1:45] **Hamish Calloway:** If you've been up to Franz Josef or Fox Glacier on the West Coast, you'll have noticed that the ice often looks blue. A lot of people assume it's reflecting the sky, but that's not it. Because the ice is so dense, it absorbs the red part of sunlight, and the blue light is what's left for us to see.
4. [2:03] **Hamish Calloway:** Okay, so how much water are we actually talking about? You'll sometimes see three-quarters quoted, but the more accurate figure is that glaciers and ice sheets hold about 70 per cent of the world's fresh water. Now, most of that isn't in places like New Zealand, or even Iceland. It's locked up in two huge ice sheets: the one covering Antarctica, which is by far the largest, and the one on Greenland.
   - heard (WER 0.027): Okay, so how much water are we actually talking about? You'll sometimes see three-quarters quoted, but the more accurate figure is that glaciers and ice sheets hold about 70% of the world's fresh water. Now, most of that isn't in places like New Zealand or even Iceland. It's locked up in two huge ice sheets, the one covering Antarctica, which is by far the largest, and the one on Greenland.
5. [2:29] **Hamish Calloway:** But it's the smaller mountain glaciers that matter most for people, because that's the water that ends up in rivers. Some researchers call them batteries, but the term you'll see most often in the literature is water towers. The idea is that they store water high up in the mountains and let it out gradually.
6. [2:47] **Hamish Calloway:** And the timing of that release is the key point. Snow and ice melt fastest in the hottest months, which tends to be exactly when there's the least rain. So glacier meltwater keeps rivers flowing during droughts, when they might otherwise drop very low or even dry up completely.
7. [3:03] **Hamish Calloway:** You may also have noticed that glacial rivers are a strange milky, turquoise colour. That's caused by tiny particles of rock which the glacier grinds up as it moves over the ground. It's sometimes loosely described as silt, but geologists call this glacial flour, and it's so fine that it stays in the water for a long way downstream.
8. [3:25] **Hamish Calloway:** Now, let's think about who uses this water. Here in Canterbury, rivers fed partly by snow and ice from the Southern Alps, the Rakaia, for example, supply irrigation schemes right across the plains. Traditionally that land was mostly used for sheep, but these days a large share of the irrigation water goes to dairy farms, which need pasture to keep growing right through summer.
9. [3:48] **Hamish Calloway:** And it's the same story, on a much bigger scale, in other parts of the world. In Central Asia, for example, rivers that start in the Pamir and Tian Shan mountains water huge areas of farmland. 10. [4:09] **Hamish Calloway:** Right, so finally, what happens as glaciers shrink? Almost all mountain glaciers are getting smaller, and New Zealand's have lost a large share of their ice since the late 1960s, sorry, the late 1970s, when regular surveys began. Now, you might expect river flow to drop straight away. But a glacier that's losing ice faster than it gains it actually releases extra water, so at first, flows increase.
   - heard (WER 0.059): Right, so finally, what happens as glaciers shrink? Almost all mountain glaciers are getting smaller, and New Zealand's have lost a large share of their ice since the late 1970s, when regular surveys began. Now, you might expect river flow to drop straight away, but a glacier that's losing ice faster than it gains it actually releases extra water. So at first, flows increase.
11. [4:37] **Hamish Calloway:** Eventually, though, the glacier gets so small that the amount of melt starts to fall. The point where meltwater reaches its maximum is called peak water, and after that, rivers get less every year. For many rivers in Asia and South America, scientists think that point has already passed, or will within the next twenty years or so.
12. [4:58] **Hamish Calloway:** There's a second danger as well. As glaciers retreat, they often leave behind lakes held back only by piles of loose rock that the ice pushed up. If one of those natural dams gives way, the water can empty out in minutes and cause sudden, destructive floods in the valleys below.
13. [5:16] **Hamish Calloway:** So, to sum up, glaciers aren't just beautiful scenery. They're a kind of savings account of water that many communities have been drawing on, and once it's spent, it isn't easily replaced. **Railways and the growth of towns: essay feedback.** You will hear a history student discussing the first draft of his essay with his tutor.

Speakers: Tom Hargreaves (male, british, history student, elevenlabs HF0hjxElqeS2vwmlzj6g); Ruth Calder (female, british, history tutor, elevenlabs FF59babHL8N8gfTgtBMT)

### Transcript

0. [0:41] **Ruth Calder:** Come in, Tom, have a seat. So, I've read the draft of your essay on early railways and the growth of towns, and overall it's a promising piece of work.
1. [0:51] **Tom Hargreaves:** Oh, that's a relief. I wasn't sure the argument really came across.
   - heard (WER 0.083): That's a relief. I wasn't sure the argument really came across.
2. [0:56] **Ruth Calder:** It mostly does. You're claiming that the railways didn't just speed up growth, they actually decided which towns grew at all. That's a bold claim, so your sources really matter. Shall we go through them one by one?
3. [1:10] **Tom Hargreaves:** Yes, please.
4. [1:11] **Ruth Calder:** Let's start with the railway company timetables. You've used them to show how often trains stopped at each town.
5. [1:19] **Tom Hargreaves:** I was a bit worried about those, to be honest. They're rather dry.
6. [1:23] **Ruth Calder:** Dry, perhaps, but they're actually the most convincing material you've got. They're hard facts about which towns were connected and when, so I'd keep them right at the centre of the essay.
7. [1:34] **Tom Hargreaves:** Great. And the census returns? I put loads of figures from those in the middle sections.
8. [1:40] **Ruth Calder:** The figures themselves are fine, they're accurate. The trouble is that whole pages of numbers interrupt your argument. Take the full tables out of the main text and put them at the back, after the conclusion. Then just mention the key figures in your paragraphs.
9. [1:55] **Tom Hargreaves:** OK. What about the newspaper reports? I quoted the Midland Courier quite a lot.
10. [2:01] **Ruth Calder:** Yes, and you need to remember who owned that paper. Several of its directors had shares in the railway company, so naturally it praised every new line. You don't have to drop it, but you should point out that it may not give a balanced picture.
11. [2:16] **Tom Hargreaves:** I hadn't thought of that. Then there's the travel diary, the one written by the engineer's wife.
12. [2:22] **Ruth Calder:** That's a lovely find. I'm not worried about bias there, actually, because she's quite critical of the railway in places. But you've copied out long passages, sometimes half a page at a time. A sentence or two would make the point.
13. [2:37] **Tom Hargreaves:** Right, I'll trim those. And the town council minutes?
14. [2:41] **Ruth Calder:** Those are useful, but you've only said they're from the 1840s, without saying which meeting. The reader needs to know exactly when each decision was taken, because timing is central to your argument. Did the council plan the new streets before or after the station opened?
15. [2:58] **Tom Hargreaves:** Ah, yes, I see. And finally, there's the textbook by Harris.
16. [3:04] **Ruth Calder:** You refer to it several times, and it's a reliable book, but I couldn't find it anywhere in your list of references at the end. Make sure it's added, with page numbers.
17. [3:14] **Tom Hargreaves:** Will do. So, what about the overall structure? I wasn't really happy with it.
18. [3:53] **Ruth Calder:** At the moment you go town by town, Swindon, then Crewe, then Wolverton, which means you repeat the same points three times. I'd rather you grouped the paragraphs by theme instead, so housing, employment, trade and so on, with examples from each town under each heading.
   - heard (WER 0.022): At the moment you go town by town – Swindon, then Crewe, then Wolverton, which means you repeat the same points three times. I'd rather you group the paragraphs by theme instead – so housing, employment, trade and so on – with examples from each town under each heading.
19. [4:11] **Tom Hargreaves:** That makes sense. It'd probably be shorter, too.
20. [4:15] **Ruth Calder:** Which brings me to length. You're about four hundred words over the limit. The section on station architecture is fine as it is, but the background section on canal companies is interesting rather than relevant. Cut that down to a single paragraph.
21. [4:30] **Tom Hargreaves:** Fair enough. I did wonder whether I'd said enough about people. It's mostly about buildings and lines.
22. [4:37] **Ruth Calder:** Exactly. I'd like to hear more about the people who actually moved to these towns. Not the managers or the engineers, you've covered them well, but the ordinary factory workers. Where did they come from, and how did they live?
23. [4:51] **Tom Hargreaves:** There's some material on that in the county archive, I think. Oh, and I was planning to put in a table of population figures.
24. [4:58] **Ruth Calder:** A table would be all right, but I think a line graph would show the trend much more clearly. You'd see the jump right after each station opened.
25. [5:07] **Tom Hargreaves:** OK, a graph it is. And the conclusion? I thought that was the strongest part.
   - heard (WER 0.067): Okay, a graph it is. And the conclusion? I thought that was the strongest part.
26. [5:13] **Ruth Calder:** It's well written, but it's rather one-sided. Some historians argue that a few of these towns were already expanding before any trains came, thanks to the turnpike roads. You should admit that, and then explain why you still think the railways were the deciding factor.
27. [5:29] **Tom Hargreaves:** That's fair. So when do you need the final version?
28. [5:33] **Ruth Calder:** The original deadline was the 21st of March, but the department has given everyone an extra week, so the new deadline is 28th March. Don't leave it until the last minute, though.
29. [5:44] **Tom Hargreaves:** I won't. Thanks, this has been really helpful.
30. [5:48] **Ruth Calder:** You're welcome, Tom. Good luck with it.

## l5|p4: succeeded

**Restoring damaged coral reefs.** You will hear a marine science lecturer giving a talk about how scientists try to restore damaged coral reefs.

Speakers: Leanne Thorburn (female, australian, university lecturer in marine science, elevenlabs w9rPM8AIZle60Nbpw7nl)

### Transcript

0. [0:43] **Leanne Thorburn:** Good morning, everyone. Last week we looked at how coral reefs form, so today I'd like to turn to something a bit more hopeful: how scientists are trying to repair reefs that have already been damaged. I'll start with why reefs decline, then talk about coral gardening, then some newer methods, and I'll finish with how we actually measure success.
1. [1:04] **Leanne Thorburn:** Right. The biggest threat by far is bleaching. Corals have tiny algae living inside their tissue, and most people think of these algae as the thing that gives coral its colour. That's true, but more importantly, they produce the bulk of the coral's food, something like ninety per cent of it. So when the water gets too warm and the coral pushes the algae out, it turns white and basically starts to starve.
   - heard (WER 0.041): Right. The biggest threat by far is bleaching. Corals have tiny algae living inside their tissue, and most people think of these algae as the thing that gives coral its color. That's true, but more importantly, they produce the bulk of the coral's food, something like 90 percent of it. So when the water gets too warm and the coral pushes the algae out, it turns white and basically starts to starve.
2. [1:31] **Leanne Thorburn:** The second threat is the crown-of-thorns starfish. Despite the dramatic name, it's simply a starfish that feeds on coral, and normally there aren't many of them around. But fertiliser washing off farms and down the rivers here in Queensland seems to help their young survive. So every few years we get huge outbreaks, and they can strip a section of reef in a matter of months.
3. [1:54] **Leanne Thorburn:** Then there are cyclones. A severe cyclone doesn't just damage individual corals; it can smash the whole structure into loose rubble. And that's a real headache, because rubble rolls around with every wave, so any baby coral that settles on it gets crushed or buried. Some sites can stay bare for decades because of that.
4. [2:14] **Leanne Thorburn:** OK, so what can we do about it? The best-known approach is coral gardening. Divers collect small fragments from healthy corals and grow them in an underwater nursery. The first nurseries were basically flat tables sitting on the seabed, but these days most teams use frames that look rather like trees, with branches made of plastic pipe, and the fragments hang from those branches in the open water.
   - heard (WER 0.014): Okay, so what can we do about it? The best-known approach is coral gardening. Divers collect small fragments from healthy corals and grow them in an underwater nursery. The first nurseries were basically flat tables sitting on the seabed, but these days most teams use frames that look rather like trees, with branches made of plastic pipe, and the fragments hang from those branches in the open water.
5. [2:40] **Leanne Thorburn:** How long do they stay in the nursery? Students often guess a few weeks, but it's a good deal longer than that. Fast-growing species might be ready in six months, but on average it's around nine months before the fragments are big enough to go back onto the reef. Then divers attach them to the reef one by one, usually with a kind of underwater cement.
6. [3:01] **Leanne Thorburn:** Now, coral gardening is sometimes criticised for being expensive. Actually, the costs have come down a lot in the last ten years. The real weakness is that it's slow. A good team might plant a few thousand corals in a season, but when you remember that the Great Barrier Reef covers an area bigger than Italy, you can see the problem.
   - heard (WER 0.016): Now, coral gardening is sometimes criticized for being expensive. Actually, the costs have come down a lot in the last 10 years. The real weakness is that it's slow. A good team might plant a few thousand corals in a season, but when you remember that the Great Barrier Reef covers an area bigger than Italy, you can see the problem.
7. [3:22] **Leanne Thorburn:** And that brings me to some newer methods. One is sometimes called coral IVF. Many corals reproduce in a mass spawning event, which happens just once a year, usually a few nights after a full moon in late spring. Researchers head out after dark and collect the eggs and sperm floating on the surface.
8. [3:42] **Leanne Thorburn:** They raise the larvae in floating pools for about a week, sorry, five days, and then release them over a damaged patch of reef. The tricky part is that larvae drift away very easily. So the team covers the area with what they call a mesh tent, which keeps the larvae in place for several days while they settle.
9. [4:01] **Leanne Thorburn:** Another line of research is breeding tougher corals. A few years ago some labs were looking at corals that could resist pollution, but the main focus now is heat. The idea is to cross corals that came through a bad bleaching year, in the hope that their offspring will cope better with warmer water.
10. [4:19] **Leanne Thorburn:** And here's one I really like. A reef isn't only coral; it needs fish, which graze on the seaweed and keep the coral clear. Healthy reefs are surprisingly noisy, with shrimps crackling and fish grunting. Researchers found that if you put underwater speakers on a damaged reef and play recordings of a healthy one, young fish are drawn to it, and a lot of them stay.
11. [4:43] **Leanne Thorburn:** Finally, how do we know if any of this actually works? That's a question people in the field have argued about for years. For a long time, projects simply reported the number of corals they'd planted. It makes a nice headline, but it tells you very little, because many of those corals might be dead a year later. So now we look at survival rates and at how much of the seabed is covered by living coral.
12. [5:08] **Leanne Thorburn:** But for me, the real test comes later. Growing bigger is good, and getting through a hot summer is even better. A restored reef is only truly successful when the corals we've put out there begin to reproduce on their own, because then the reef can start rebuilding itself without our help.
13. [5:26] **Leanne Thorburn:** Right, that's all for today. For next week, please read the chapter on reef monitoring, and have a look at the photos from our own nursery off Heron Island, which I've put online.

  - ru: Holly удивлена тем, что студенты думают, будто спят намного больше, чем на самом деле, и что память всё ещё страдает через три дня после одной бессонной ночи. В прочитанном тексте говорилось, что короткий дневной сон не вредит. А о том, что исследование проводилось на студентах университета, говорит Sam, а не Holly, и его это не удивило.
  - uk: Holly здивована тим, що студенти думають, ніби сплять набагато більше, ніж насправді, і що пам'ять досі страждає через три дні після однієї безсонної ночі. У прочитаному тексті йшлося про те, що короткий денний сон не шкодить. А про те, що дослідження проводили на студентах університету, згадує Sam, а не Holly, і його це не здивувало.

**multiple_choice_multi** — Choose TWO letters, A–E, for each question.
- A: students' paid work was not considered
- B: all participants came from one country
- C: the samples were too small to be useful
- D: sleep was self-reported, not measured
- E: the studies were too old to be reliable

- **23-24** Which TWO weaknesses do the students identify in the research? → **A | D** (solvers right / right; controller A,D)
  - evidence: "a lot of it depends on students saying how long they slept. Nobody actually measured it."
  - en: Sam complains that sleep was not measured but reported by students in a questionnaire, and Holly says none of the studies asked about evening jobs. The samples had several hundred people and the studies are recent, so these are not weaknesses.
  - ru: Sam жалуется, что сон не измеряли: студенты сами сообщали о нём в анкете. Holly отмечает, что ни в одном из исследований не спрашивали о работе по вечерам. В выборках было по несколько сотен человек, а сами исследования недавние, так что это не недостатки.
  - uk: Sam скаржиться, що сон не вимірювали: студенти самі повідомляли про нього в анкеті. Holly зауважує, що в жодному з досліджень не питали про роботу ввечері. У вибірках було по кількасот осіб, а самі дослідження нещодавні, тож це не недоліки.

**matching_features** — What do the students say each study found? Choose the correct letter, A–I, for each study. You will not need all the letters.
- A: higher grades linked to a consistent bedtime
- B: weaker recall after revising all night
- C: no connection between sleep length and grades
- D: better problem-solving after a daytime nap
- E: more calculation errors after missing sleep
- F: sleep quality mattering more than sleep quantity
- G: late phone use linked to shorter sleep
- H: regular exercise linked to better sleep
- I: weekend lie-ins failing to make up for lost sleep

- **25** The Auckland study of first-year students → **A** (solvers A / A; controller A)
  - evidence: "The regular group ended up with better marks, even though the total hours they slept were about the same."
  - en: Students who went to bed at about the same time every night got better marks. The number of hours was similar, so the key factor was the regular bedtime.
  - ru: Студенты, которые каждый вечер ложились спать примерно в одно и то же время, получали более высокие оценки. Количество часов сна было примерно одинаковым, поэтому главным фактором было именно регулярное время отхода ко сну.
  - uk: Студенти, які щовечора лягали спати приблизно в один і той самий час, отримували вищі оцінки. Кількість годин сну була приблизно однаковою, тож ключовим чинником був саме регулярний час відходу до сну.
- **26** The study of trainee nurses → **E** (solvers E / E; controller E)
  - evidence: "they made far more mistakes when working out drug doses"
  - en: After a night shift the nurses made more mistakes calculating drug doses. Their memory for patient details was fine, so it was not about recall.
  - ru: После ночной смены медсёстры чаще ошибались при расчёте доз лекарств. С памятью на данные пациентов всё было в порядке, так что дело было не в запоминании.
  - uk: Після нічної зміни медсестри частіше помилялися, розраховуючи дози ліків. З пам'яттю на дані пацієнтів усе було гаразд, тож річ була не в запам'ятовуванні.
- **27** The German laboratory study → **D** (solvers D / D; controller D)
  - evidence: "the nap group were much quicker at spotting a hidden rule in a puzzle afterwards"
  - en: The group who napped solved a puzzle faster. Both groups remembered the same number of words, so the finding was about problem-solving, not memory.
  - ru: Группа, которая поспала днём, быстрее решила головоломку. Обе группы запомнили одинаковое количество слов, так что результат касался решения задач, а не памяти.
  - uk: Група, яка поспала вдень, швидше розв'язала головоломку. Обидві групи запам'ятали однакову кількість слів, тож результат стосувався розв'язання задач, а не пам'яті.
- **28** The American online survey → **C** (solvers C / C; controller C)
  - evidence: "how long people slept made no difference to their grades at all"
  - en: This survey found that the amount of sleep did not affect grades, which is why Holly calls it the odd one out.
  - ru: Этот опрос показал, что количество сна не влияло на оценки, поэтому Holly называет его "the odd one out" — исключением из общего ряда.
  - uk: Це опитування показало, що кількість сну не впливала на оцінки, тому Holly називає його "the odd one out" — винятком із загального ряду.
- **29** The Wellington study of secondary school pupils → **G** (solvers G / G; controller G)
  - evidence: "pupils who were on their phones after eleven at night got about forty minutes less sleep"
  - en: Using phones late at night meant pupils slept about forty minutes less. The study also asked about exercise, but that showed nothing much.
  - ru: Из-за того, что школьники пользовались телефонами поздно вечером, они спали примерно на сорок минут меньше. В исследовании также спрашивали о физических нагрузках, но это почти ничего не показало.
  - uk: Через те, що школярі користувалися телефонами пізно ввечері, вони спали приблизно на сорок хвилин менше. У дослідженні також питали про фізичні навантаження, але це майже нічого не показало.
- **30** The Japanese study → **I** (solvers I / I; controller I)
  - evidence: "They still did worse in Monday's tests, so the extra hours didn't make up for it."
  - en: Students who slept in at the weekend after losing sleep during the week still did worse, so the extra weekend sleep did not repair the loss.
  - ru: Студенты, которые отсыпались в выходные после недосыпа в течение недели, всё равно учились хуже, так что дополнительный сон в выходные не восполнял потерю.
  - uk: Студенти, які відсипалися на вихідних після недосипання протягом тижня, все одно навчалися гірше, тож додатковий сон на вихідних не компенсував втрати.

## l6|p4: succeeded

**The history of papermaking.** You will hear a university lecturer giving a talk about how paper has been made over the centuries.

Speakers: Edward Lockwood (male, british, university lecturer in the history of technology, elevenlabs HF0hjxElqeS2vwmlzj6g)

### Transcript

0. [0:42] **Edward Lockwood:** Good morning, everyone. I'm Dr Edward Lockwood, and today we're continuing our series on everyday technologies with something you probably take for granted: paper. I'll begin with how it was first made, then look at how the craft spread westwards, and I'll finish with the machines of the nineteenth century and recycling today.
1. [1:00] **Edward Lockwood:** Right. Before paper, people wrote on all sorts of surfaces. The Egyptians used papyrus, which is where our word 'paper' comes from, and in China people wrote on strips of bamboo or on silk. The trouble was that bamboo was heavy and silk was very expensive, so there was a real need for something better.
2. [1:19] **Edward Lockwood:** The traditional date for the invention of paper is the year 105, when a court official called Cai Lun presented the process to the emperor. Archaeologists have since found older fragments, so he probably improved the method rather than inventing it. Let's go through the process step by step, as it was carried out in his time.
3. [1:38] **Edward Lockwood:** First, the raw materials were collected: the inner bark of the mulberry tree, hemp, old rags and even worn-out fishing nets. These were chopped into small pieces and left to soak in water. Now, you might expect salt to be added at this point, but in fact what they added was lime, which helped to break the material down.
4. [1:57] **Edward Lockwood:** Next, the softened material was beaten with wooden hammers. This was hard, tiring work, and it could go on for hours, until the fibres separated and formed a thick pulp. That was mixed with plenty of water in a large vat. Then came the clever part. The papermaker dipped a screen into the vat and lifted it out, covered in a thin layer of fibres. The earliest screens were made of cloth, but cloth tended to sag, so they soon switched to bamboo, which kept the sheet perfectly flat.
5. [2:26] **Edward Lockwood:** The water drained away through the screen, and the wet sheet was pressed to squeeze out the rest. Some workshops hung their sheets on lines, rather like washing, but the usual method was to brush each one onto a heated wall, where it dried in just a few minutes. And the last stage? Once it was completely dry, the sheet was polished with a smooth stone, so that ink wouldn't spread across it. Some makers tried shells for this, but stone gave a better finish.
6. [3:25] **Edward Lockwood:** OK, so how did this knowledge leave China? For several centuries it was kept fairly secret. The usual story is that it reached Samarkand, in Central Asia, in 751, after a battle in which Chinese papermakers were taken prisoner. Samarkand had clean rivers, but plenty of towns had those. What really made it successful was the flax grown all around the city, which gave a steady supply of fibre.
7. [3:50] **Edward Lockwood:** From there, the craft moved to Baghdad, where a paper mill was working by the 790s. Government offices were quick to adopt it. Until then, official records had been kept on parchment, made from animal skin, and paper replaced it partly because it was cheaper, but mainly because anything scraped off paper left a visible mark, so documents were much harder to forge.
8. [4:12] **Edward Lockwood:** Papermaking then crossed North Africa and reached Spain in the twelfth century, and Italy shortly afterwards. The Italian mills, especially in a town called Fabriano, made several improvements. They used animal glue to give the paper a harder surface, which is interesting, but the innovation I'd like you to note down is the watermark. These were faint designs pressed into each sheet, so that buyers could tell which mill had produced it.
9. [4:36] **Edward Lockwood:** Right, let's jump to the nineteenth century. By around 1800, demand was growing fast because of newspapers and the spread of schooling, but European paper was still made from rags, and there simply weren't enough of them to go round. Some mills even put up notices asking households to save their old clothes.
10. [4:54] **Edward Lockwood:** Hand production was also slow. A skilled team might manage a few thousand sheets a day. The answer was the machine developed in England by the Fourdrinier brothers and patented in 1806. Instead of producing separate sheets, it made paper as one continuous roll, which could then be cut to whatever size the customer wanted.
11. [5:14] **Edward Lockwood:** That still left the problem of raw materials. From the 1840s, the solution was wood pulp, which was cheap and almost unlimited. But this new paper had a serious flaw. It contained acid from the production process, and over time that acid makes paper turn yellow and brittle. British libraries now spend millions of pounds trying to rescue books from this period, while much older rag paper is often in far better condition.
12. [5:39] **Edward Lockwood:** Finally, recycling. A lot of people believe paper can be recycled forever, but it can't. Each time it goes through the process, the fibres get shorter and weaker. You'll see figures of five times quoted, but most experts put the limit at around seven, after which the fibres are too short to bond together.
13. [5:58] **Edward Lockwood:** People also worry about ink, but that's actually removed quite easily, using soap and air bubbles that carry it to the surface. The real headache for recycling plants is grease from things like takeaway pizza boxes, because it can't be separated from the fibres, and it ruins a whole batch. Next week, we'll look at printing.

  - uk: Лектор каже, що можна було б очікувати "salt", але насправді додавали "lime", щоб матеріал легше розкладався.
- **32** Material beaten with wooden hammers to make a thick ______ → **pulp** (solvers pulp / pulp; controller pulp)
  - evidence: "until the fibres separated and formed a thick pulp"
  - en: The material was beaten for hours until the fibres separated and became a thick pulp.

- **36** Baghdad: paper replaced ______ for official records, as it was harder to forge → **parchment** (solvers parchment / parchment; controller parchment)
  - evidence: "official records had been kept on parchment, made from animal skin"
  - en: Government records had been kept on parchment, and paper replaced it. Papyrus was mentioned earlier, but for Egypt, not Baghdad.
  - ru: Государственные записи раньше вели на "parchment", и бумага его заменила. "Papyrus" упоминался раньше, но в связи с Египтом, а не с Багдадом.
  - uk: Державні записи раніше вели на "parchment", і папір його замінив. "Papyrus" згадували раніше, але щодо Єгипту, а не Багдада.

  - evidence: "the innovation I'd like you to note down is the watermark"

  - uk: Використовували й тваринний клей, але лектор просить студентів звернути увагу на "watermark" — водяний знак, за яким можна було визначити паперову фабрику.
- **38** Around 1800: demand rose, but there was a shortage of ______ → **rags** (solvers rags / rags; controller rags)
  - evidence: "European paper was still made from rags, and there simply weren't enough of them to go round"

## l7|p2: succeeded

**Safety talk for new trampers.** You will hear the leader of a tramping club talking to a group of new members about staying safe in the hills.

Speakers: Dougal Fraser (male, new_zealand, hiking club leader, elevenlabs 1HGaTm3UFxAyxae5H0GU)

### Transcript

0. [0:42] **Dougal Fraser:** Kia ora everyone, and welcome along. I'm Dougal Fraser, and I've been leading trips for the Canterbury Ridge Tramping Club for about twelve years now. Tonight's all about staying safe out in the hills, so grab a cuppa and get comfy.
1. [0:55] **Dougal Fraser:** First, a quick word about your first trip with us. Normally we'd take new members up to Arthur's Pass, but there are roadworks on the highway all month, so that's off. We do evening walks on the Port Hills, but those are for people who've already been out with us. So your first one will be over on Banks Peninsula, on Saturday the twenty-third.
2. [1:15] **Dougal Fraser:** Right, planning. When people plan a tramp, they tend to spend ages on how hard the track's graded, or on sorting out who's driving. Both matter, sure. But the one thing I insist on, above everything else, is that you leave details of your route and the time you expect to be out by. No exceptions.
   - heard (WER 0.054): Right, planning. When people plan a trip, they tend to spend ages on how hard the tracks are rated, or on sorting out who's driving. Both matter, sure, but the one thing I insist on above everything else is that you leave details of your route and the time you expect to be out by. No exceptions.
3. [1:34] **Dougal Fraser:** Now, who do you leave those details with? Years ago we used to drop a form in at the Department of Conservation visitor centre, but they stopped accepting them a while back. And please don't send it to our club secretary, because she can't be by the phone around the clock. Give it to a friend or someone in your family, someone you trust to raise the alarm if you're not back.
   - heard (WER 0.014): Now, who do you leave those details with? Years ago we used to drop a form in at the Department of Conservation visitor center, but they stopped accepting them a while back. And please don't send it to our club secretary, because she can't be by the phone around the clock. Give it to a friend or someone in your family, someone you trust to raise the alarm if you're not back.
4. [1:55] **Dougal Fraser:** Okay, equipment, starting with your feet. The club has a few rain jackets members can borrow, but we haven't got any boots, sorry. And I'm not going to inspect everyone's footwear at the car park either. What I will ask is that you don't turn up in boots straight out of the box. Wear them around town for a couple of weeks first, so they're properly broken in.
5. [2:15] **Dougal Fraser:** Next, the weather, which in this part of the country can change in minutes. Lots of you will just check the weather app on your phone. The club website does post a forecast link too, but it's often a day out of date. The one we rely on is the mountain forecast on MetService, because it's specific to the high country.
6. [2:34] **Dougal Fraser:** So what happens if the weather turns on us halfway through a trip? Most people assume we'd just turn round and go back the way we came. Actually, that's often not the safest choice, because you'd be heading back over exposed ridges. Sitting it out and waiting isn't great either, as you get cold really fast. What we normally do is drop down to a lower track, an escape route we'll have worked out in advance.
7. [2:58] **Dougal Fraser:** And rivers. Crossings are one of the biggest risks for trampers in this country. Our rule used to be that you didn't cross if the water was above waist height, but that's far too deep when it's flowing fast. So now it's simple: if it's higher than your knees, nobody goes in. People ask whether linking arms makes it okay, and it helps, but it doesn't change the rule.
8. [3:21] **Dougal Fraser:** Right, let's talk about what goes in your pack. The club carries a personal locator beacon and the main first-aid kit, and the trip leader looks after both, so you don't need your own.
9. [4:05] **Dougal Fraser:** But every single person must bring a head torch, even on a day walk, because delays happen. You'll also need a spare warm layer, like a fleece, however sunny it looks when you set off. A paper map's handy, but I'll always have one, so that's up to you.
10. [4:21] **Dougal Fraser:** Now, emergencies. If someone gets hurt, don't move them unless they're in danger where they are. And don't give them anything to eat or drink yet, until we know how bad it is. What you should do is get something underneath them and keep them warm, because the cold's the real danger out there. Then, if it's serious, we set off the beacon, which sends our exact position to the rescue services.
11. [4:45] **Dougal Fraser:** In the old days, you'd send one person running out to get help. We don't do that any more. We keep the group together, and nobody heads off on their own.
   - heard (WER 0.065): In the old days, you'd send one person running out to get help. We don't do that anymore. We keep the group together and nobody heads off on their own.
12. [4:53] **Dougal Fraser:** Last thing: next Thursday's session. We'd hoped to have a volunteer from LandSAR, the search and rescue people, but they can't come till next month. So instead we'll head out onto the field behind the hall for some map and compass practice. And I'll give you a proper demonstration of how the beacon works, since any of you might need to set it off one day.
   - heard (WER 0.015): Last thing, next Thursday's session. We'd hoped to have a volunteer from LANSAR, the search and rescue people, but they can't come till next month. So, instead, we'll head out onto the field behind the hall for some map and compass practice, and I'll give you a proper demonstration of how the beacon works, since any of you might need to set it off one day.
13. [5:13] **Dougal Fraser:** The first-aid course is separate, by the way. That's a weekend in March, and it costs $85. And no, sorry, there's no gear sale this year. Right, any questions?

Speakers: Chloe Maddigan (female, australian, environmental science student, elevenlabs w9rPM8AIZle60Nbpw7nl); Lachlan Petrie (male, australian, environmental science student, elevenlabs WLKp2jV6nrS8aMkPPDRO); Dr Russell Ambrose (male, north_american, environmental science lecturer, xai sal)

### Transcript

0. [0:43] **Dr Russell Ambrose:** Come in, Chloe, Lachlan. Have a seat. So you wanted to talk about your report on the Millers Creek wetland trip?
1. [0:49] **Chloe Maddigan:** Yes, thanks, Dr Ambrose. We've got a draft of the observations section, but we wanted to check we're on the right track before we go any further.
2. [0:58] **Dr Russell Ambrose:** Sure. Walk me through what you saw. Start at the beginning.
3. [1:02] **Lachlan Petrie:** Okay, so we started on the boardwalk near the car park. We'd expected to hear frogs along there, because the guidebook mentions them, but it was totally silent. What we did notice was heaps of chip packets and drink bottles jammed under the planks. People obviously just drop them on the way in.
   - heard (WER 0.075): OK, so we started on the boardwalk near the carpark. We'd expected to hear frogs along there because the guidebook mentions them, but it was totally silent. What we did notice was heaps of chip packets and drink bottles jammed under the planks. People obviously just dropped them on the way in.
4. [1:20] **Dr Russell Ambrose:** That's pretty common near access points. And then?
5. [1:23] **Chloe Maddigan:** Then we went to the bird hide. Lachlan was convinced he'd spotted a dead fish floating near it.
6. [1:29] **Lachlan Petrie:** It turned out to be a bit of bark.
7. [1:32] **Chloe Maddigan:** Right. But the exciting thing was a pair of swamp harriers, no, sorry, purple swamphens, sitting on a nest in the rushes just in front of the hide. We watched them for about twenty minutes.
8. [1:45] **Dr Russell Ambrose:** Great. What about the inlet channel, where the creek comes in?
9. [1:48] **Lachlan Petrie:** We took the temperature there because we assumed it'd be warmer, with the water being so shallow, but the readings were completely normal. The water itself, though, was a sort of brown colour, really thick with sediment. It'd rained heavily the night before, so that probably washed soil in from the farms upstream.
   - heard (WER 0.057): We took the temperature there because we assumed it'd be warmer with the water being so shallow, but the readings were completely normal. 10. [2:07] **Dr Russell Ambrose:** Probably, yes. You'll want to mention the rainfall in the report.
11. [2:11] **Chloe Maddigan:** Okay. Then the reed beds, which were the highlight for me. As soon as we got close, the croaking was unbelievable. We counted well over forty of them in a ten-metre stretch.

12. [2:23] **Dr Russell Ambrose:** Wow. And did you get down to the mudflats?
13. [2:27] **Lachlan Petrie:** We did. We'd been told to look out for an introduced weed there, but we couldn't find any at all. Instead there were several fish lying on the mud, and they weren't moving. Maybe five or six of them.
14. [2:39] **Dr Russell Ambrose:** Hmm, that could be low oxygen. Note it, but don't speculate too much.

## l7|p4: succeeded

**Urban heat islands.** You will hear a lecturer in urban planning giving a talk about why cities become hotter than the surrounding countryside and what can be done about it.

Speakers: Carolyn Mercer (female, north_american, university lecturer in urban planning, xai ara)

### Transcript

0. [0:45] **Carolyn Mercer:** Good morning, everyone. Today we're moving on from transportation to something that affects almost every city on this continent, and that's what's known as the urban heat island. Put simply, it's the way built-up areas end up noticeably warmer than the countryside around them.
1. [1:00] **Carolyn Mercer:** On a summer day, a downtown core can be several degrees hotter than nearby farmland. Now, most people assume the difference is biggest in the middle of the afternoon, when the sun's at its strongest. In fact, that's not really the case. The gap between city and countryside is usually widest at night, because the city keeps releasing the heat it stored up during the day, while rural areas cool down fast.
2. [1:22] **Carolyn Mercer:** So why does this happen? Well, the first reason is the materials we build with. Brick and concrete both soak up heat, but the real culprit is asphalt, the dark material on our roads and parking lots. On a sunny afternoon, it can get hot enough to burn your hand.
3. [1:37] **Carolyn Mercer:** The second reason is the lack of vegetation. Plants draw water up from the soil and release it through their leaves, and as that water turns to vapor, it takes heat out of the air. This process, evaporation, is basically nature's air conditioning. Replace a meadow with a shopping mall, and you lose it.
4. [1:54] **Carolyn Mercer:** Third, there's the shape of the city itself. When tall buildings line narrow streets, you get what planners call canyons. Sunlight bounces from wall to wall instead of escaping, and the wind can't get through to carry the warm air away.
5. [2:08] **Carolyn Mercer:** And finally, there's waste heat from cars, from factories, and, rather ironically, from air conditioners pumping hot air out onto the street. So that's four causes, all working together.
6. [2:19] **Carolyn Mercer:** Okay, so what are the effects? Let's start with health. Heat waves are one of the deadliest kinds of extreme weather in North America, and the people most at risk aren't always the ones you'd expect.
7. [2:30] **Carolyn Mercer:** Young children are vulnerable, yes, but the biggest danger is to older people who live alone, often in top-floor apartments, with nobody checking on them. Many of them don't realize how dangerous the heat is until it's too late.
8. [2:43] **Carolyn Mercer:** Heat also makes air quality worse. Higher temperatures speed up the chemical reactions that produce ground-level ozone, which irritates the lungs and is especially bad for people with asthma. So on the hottest days, the air is also at its most harmful to breathe.
9. [2:57] **Carolyn Mercer:** Then there's energy. When it's hot, everybody turns up the air conditioning at the same time, usually between about four and, well, seven in the evening. Electricity bills go up, of course, but the bigger worry is the power grid. If demand gets too high, the utilities simply can't keep up, and that's when you see blackouts, which switch off the very air conditioning that vulnerable people depend on.
10. [3:19] **Carolyn Mercer:** Right. So what can cities do about all this? The most obvious answer is trees. A mature tree shades the ground and cools the air through its leaves, as we discussed.
11. [3:29] **Carolyn Mercer:** But where you plant it really matters. A lot of homeowners think the south side of a building is best. Actually, research shows the biggest savings come from trees on the west side, because they block the low, intense sun of late afternoon. That's exactly when buildings are at their hottest.
12. [3:45] **Carolyn Mercer:** The challenge is that city streets aren't a great place for a tree to grow. Under the sidewalk, the soil is packed hard and full of pipes and cables, so there's very little room for roots to spread. That's why so many street trees die young, and why some cities now build special underground frames that hold loose soil beneath the pavement.
13. [4:03] **Carolyn Mercer:** The second solution is what we call cool roofs. A normal dark roof can reach over 150 degrees Fahrenheit in summer. A cool roof is covered with a reflective coating, usually white, that sends most of the sunlight straight back into the sky. New York, for example, has had volunteers treating thousands of rooftops this way.
14. [4:21] **Carolyn Mercer:** Cool roofs can go on almost any building, but they're cheapest and simplest on flat roofs, the kind you see on warehouses, schools and apartment blocks. On a steep roof, the work's harder and more dangerous. And frankly, a lot of homeowners just don't like the look of a bright white roof on their house.
15. [4:38] **Carolyn Mercer:** There is one drawback, though. In places with cold winters, like Chicago or Toronto, a cool roof reflects away sunlight you'd actually like to have in January. So your heating costs can go up slightly in the colder months. In most cases, though, the summer savings are far bigger than that winter penalty.
16. [4:55] **Carolyn Mercer:** Okay, I'll stop there for today. For next week, I'd like you to look at a map of your own neighborhood and think about where the hottest spots might be, and which of these solutions would suit them. We'll compare notes in the seminar.

  - uk: Рослини виділяють воду через листя, і її випаровування ("evaporation") охолоджує повітря. У містах, де мало рослин, такого охолодження немає.
