### Questions

**form_completion** — Complete the form below. Write ONE WORD AND/OR A NUMBER for each answer. (Rental Enquiry Form)

- **1** Caller's name: Josh ______ → **Templeton** (solvers Templeton / Templeton; controller Templeton)

- **2** Address of flat: 22 ______ Road → **Wattle** (solvers Wattle / Wattle; controller Wattle)
  - evidence: "it's 22 Wattle Road. That's W-A-T-T-L-E."
  - en: The caller says the flat is at 22 Wattle Road and spells the street name.
  - ru: Звонящий говорит, что квартира находится по адресу "22 Wattle Road", и диктует название улицы по буквам.
  - uk: Абонент каже, що квартира розташована за адресою "22 Wattle Road", і диктує назву вулиці по літерах.
- **3** Floor: ______ → **third | 3rd** (solvers third / third; controller third)
  - evidence: "This one's on the third floor"
  - en: The agent first says the second floor, but corrects herself: that is the flat next door. This flat is on the third floor.
  - ru: Агент сначала говорит "second floor", но тут же поправляет себя: это соседняя квартира. Нужная квартира находится на "third floor".
  - uk: Агентка спочатку каже "second floor", але одразу виправляється: це сусідня квартира. Потрібна квартира розташована на "third floor".
- **4** Rent per week: $______ → **485** (solvers 485 / 485; controller 485)
  - evidence: "so it's now $485 a week"
  - en: The advert said $460, but the agent explains that was the old price. The rent is now $485 a week.
  - ru: В объявлении было указано "$460", но агент объясняет, что это старая цена. Сейчас аренда стоит "$485" в неделю.
  - uk: В оголошенні було зазначено "$460", але агентка пояснює, що це стара ціна. Тепер оренда коштує "$485" на тиждень.
- **5** Bill included in rent: ______ → **water** (solvers water / water; controller water)
  - evidence: "the owner pays the water bill, so that one's included in the rent"
  - en: Electricity and gas must be paid by the tenant, but the owner pays for water, so it is included.
  - ru: За электричество и газ платит арендатор, а за воду ("water") — владелец, поэтому вода включена в стоимость.
  - uk: За електрику й газ платить орендар, а за воду ("water") — власник, тому вода входить у вартість.

**table_completion** — Complete the table below. Write NO MORE THAN TWO WORDS AND/OR A NUMBER for each answer. (Local area)

- **6** Tram stop | 2 minutes' walk | Journey to city: ______ minutes → **25 | twenty-five** (solvers 25 / 25; controller 25)
  - evidence: "the tram gets you into the city in about 25 minutes"
  - en: The tram takes about 25 minutes. The 15 minutes mentioned is the time for the train, not the tram.
  - ru: Поездка на трамвае ("tram") занимает около "25" минут. Упомянутые "15" минут — это время в пути на поезде ("train"), а не на трамвае.
  - uk: Поїздка трамваєм ("tram") триває близько "25" хвилин. Згадані "15" хвилин — це час у дорозі потягом ("train"), а не трамваєм.
- **7** Supermarket | On Sydney Road, opposite the ______ | Open until 10 pm → **library** (solvers library / library; controller library)

- **8** Park | 5 minutes' walk | Tennis courts and outdoor ______ → **swimming pool** (solvers swimming pool / swimming pool; controller swimming pool)

  - en: The park has tennis courts and an outdoor swimming pool. The running track is no longer there because it became a playground.

  - uk: У парку є тенісні корти ("tennis courts") і відкритий басейн ("outdoor swimming pool"). Бігової доріжки ("running track") більше немає: на її місці тепер дитячий майданчик ("playground").

I'll start with the ground floor, and then I'll tell you about the new galleries upstairs.
1. [1:02] **Owen Ratcliffe:** Right, you've all got a copy of the ground-floor plan, I hope. We're standing just inside the main entrance, facing north. On your left is the ticket desk, and on your right is the shop.
2. [1:13] **Owen Ratcliffe:** Now, most of you will want somewhere to leave coats and bags. The room beside the ticket desk, to the west, looks as if it might be the cloakroom, but it's actually staff offices. The cloakroom's on the other side, right next to the shop, just beyond it to the east.
3. [1:28] **Owen Ratcliffe:** Then, if you walk straight up the main corridor, you'll come to a crossroads where it meets the long corridor running east to west. Just before that crossroads, there are two large rooms facing each other. The one on your left is the Energy Zone, which, I'm sorry to say, won't open until the summer. The one on your right is the Flight Gallery, with the old biplane hanging from the ceiling, and that's open now.

Just before that crossroads there are two large rooms facing each other. The one on your left is the Energy Zone, which I'm sorry to say won't open until the summer. The one on your right is the Flight Gallery, with the old bi-plane hanging from the ceiling, and that's open now.
4. [1:51] **Owen Ratcliffe:** OK. At the crossroads, turn left and walk west, right to the end of the corridor. The room on your left, at the very end, is where we'll hold our temporary exhibitions. The first one's about volcanoes, and it's well worth a look. It runs until the end of September, and there's no extra charge for it.
5. [2:07] **Owen Ratcliffe:** Directly opposite it, on the right-hand side, is the picnic room. If you've brought your own sandwiches, that's the place to eat them. Please don't eat them in the café, which is just north of the picnic room. The café staff get rather upset about that!
6. [2:21] **Owen Ratcliffe:** Now, for families with small children. The play area used to be in the basement, but we've moved it up here. It's the long, narrow room between the café and the lifts. The lifts are at the far end of the main corridor, by the way, so if you're pushing a buggy, that's the easiest way to reach it.
   - heard (WER 0.017): Now for families with small children: the play area used to be in the basement, but we've moved it up here. It's the long narrow room between the cafe and the lifts. The lifts are at the far end of the main corridor by the way, so if you're pushing a buggy that's the easiest way to reach it.
7. [2:37] **Owen Ratcliffe:** And finally, the toilets. Go back to the crossroads, and this time follow the long corridor east instead of west. Right at the end, the door on your right is the toilets. Opposite them, on your left, is the lecture theatre, where there are free talks every afternoon.
8. [2:53] **Owen Ratcliffe:** Right, that's the ground floor. Now for the exciting part: the six brand-new galleries on the first floor. Every one of them has something hands-on, so let me quickly tell you what you can actually do in each.
9. [3:38] **Owen Ratcliffe:** Let's start with the Space Gallery. People always ask me if there's a telescope they can look through. I'm afraid there isn't; there simply wasn't room. But you can climb into a full-size copy of a space capsule, and with the screens and controls inside, it feels as if you're really docking with the space station.
10. [3:56] **Owen Ratcliffe:** Next door is the Medicine Gallery. Most of it's glass cases, as you'd expect, but there's a table in the middle where you can put on gloves and pick up genuine surgical tools from the 1800s. They're surprisingly heavy.
11. [4:08] **Owen Ratcliffe:** Then there's the Engineering Hall. You'll see a big robot arm in there, and children always want a go with it, but it's behind glass, so nobody can operate it, I'm afraid. What you can do is put together a little bridge out of card and straws, and then add weights to find out how much it'll hold before it falls down.
   - heard (WER 0.016): Then there's the engineering hall. You'll see a big robot arm in there, and children always want to go with it, but it's behind glass so nobody can operate it I'm afraid. 12. [4:26] **Owen Ratcliffe:** The Sound Studio is great fun, and it's especially popular with teenagers. There's a booth where you talk into a microphone, and it keeps a copy of what you've said so you can listen to yourself afterwards. Most people are shocked by what they sound like!
13. [4:40] **Owen Ratcliffe:** On the Chemistry Stage, you can't touch anything, for obvious safety reasons. But at 11 am, 1 pm and 3 pm each day, one of our scientists mixes chemicals in front of the audience, and you can watch the colours change and the smoke appear.
14. [4:55] **Owen Ratcliffe:** And last of all, the Climate Gallery. Along one wall there are touch screens with ten questions about the weather and the planet, and at the end, it tells you how many you got right. See if you can beat your children's score! ## l8|p3: succeeded

**Choosing a research method.** You will hear two psychology students discussing how to collect data for a research project on exam stress.

Speakers: Brianna Castillo (female, north_american, psychology student, xai ara); Jordan Whitfield (male, north_american, psychology student, xai sal)

### Transcript

0. [0:42] **Brianna Castillo:** Hi Jordan. Okay, so Professor Reyes wants our method proposal by Friday, and we still haven't picked between interviews and a questionnaire. We really need to decide today.
1. [0:53] **Jordan Whitfield:** Yeah, I know. I've been going back and forth on it. What's your feeling?
2. [0:57] **Brianna Castillo:** Honestly, I keep coming back to interviews. Everyone says they give you richer, more detailed data, and that's true. But for me the big thing is that you can follow up. If someone says they just deal with stress, you can ask them to explain what they actually mean.
3. [1:12] **Jordan Whitfield:** Right. And people probably feel more relaxed chatting than filling in a form, too.
4. [1:17] **Brianna Castillo:** Hmm, I'm not so sure about that, actually. A lot of students would find it pretty awkward talking to a stranger about stress. Anyway, what about you? You were leaning toward a questionnaire, weren't you?

**How migrating birds navigate.** You will hear a zoology lecturer giving a talk about how birds find their way when they migrate.

Speakers: Rebecca Sutherland (female, new_zealand, university lecturer in zoology, elevenlabs 7ceZgj78jCCeAW93ItNk)

### Transcript

0. [0:42] **Rebecca Sutherland:** Good morning, everyone. Today we're carrying on with animal behaviour, and I want to look at one of the big puzzles in zoology: how migrating birds find their way. Think of the bar-tailed godwit. Every September it leaves Alaska and flies non-stop to places like the Firth of Thames, here in New Zealand, a journey of over eleven thousand kilometres. So how on earth does it know where it's going?
   - heard (WER 0.056): Good morning everyone. Today we're carrying on with animal behavior, and I want to look at one of the big puzzles in zoology: how migrating birds find their way. Think of the bar-tailed godwit. Every September, it leaves Alaska and flies nonstop to places like the Firth of Thames here in New Zealand, a journey of over 11,000 kilometers. So how on earth does it know where it's going?
1. [1:08] **Rebecca Sutherland:** Let's start with the sun. In the 1950s a German scientist, Gustav Kramer, noticed something odd about caged starlings. When the season for migrating came round, they didn't sit still. They fluttered and hopped about, and they kept facing the direction they'd normally fly in. This is what we now call migratory restlessness, and it gave researchers a really handy way to measure which way a bird wants to go.
2. [1:36] **Rebecca Sutherland:** Kramer then asked whether the birds were actually using the sun. He thought about moving the cage, or putting lamps around it, but in the end he went for something simpler. He used mirrors to make the sun appear to be in a different position. And sure enough, the starlings turned to face a new direction, by just about the same amount he'd shifted the sun.
   - heard (WER 0.015): Kreimer then asked whether the birds were actually using the sun. He thought about moving the cage, or putting lamps around it, but in the end he went for something simpler: he used mirrors to make the sun appear to be in a different position. And sure enough, the starlings turned to face a new direction, by just about the same amount he'd shifted the sun.
3. [1:58] **Rebecca Sutherland:** Now, there's a complication here. The sun moves across the sky during the day, so a bird can't just fly towards it. It has to adjust for the time, and to do that it needs an internal clock. Researchers showed this by keeping birds under artificial lights that were switched on six hours late. Their clocks were thrown out, and they headed off in the wrong direction, just as you'd predict.
4. [2:23] **Rebecca Sutherland:** But lots of birds migrate at night. So what about the stars? In the 1960s an American researcher, Stephen Emlen, studied a small songbird called the indigo bunting. People often assume he took them to an observatory, but no, he tested them inside a planetarium, where he could control exactly which stars they saw.
5. [2:45] **Rebecca Sutherland:** What he found was fascinating. Young buntings don't come with a star map built in. Instead, as they grow, they learn which part of the sky the stars turn around, the centre of rotation, which in the northern hemisphere is close to the Pole Star. And when Emlen made the planetarium sky rotate around a different star, the birds treated that star as north.
6. [3:09] **Rebecca Sutherland:** Right, the third cue is the earth's magnetic field. In the 1960s Wolfgang Wiltschko put European robins in cages surrounded by electric coils, which let him change the magnetic field. The robins changed direction too. But here's the surprising part. Robins don't sense whether the field points north or south, the way a compass needle does. What they detect is the angle of the field lines in relation to the ground.
7. [4:11] **Rebecca Sutherland:** So where's the sensor? For a long time people thought it was tiny bits of iron in the beak. That idea hasn't really held up, I'm afraid. The current theory is that it involves light-sensitive proteins in the eye, which may mean that birds can actually see the magnetic field in some way.
8. [4:29] **Rebecca Sutherland:** There's a nice example of how sensitive this system is. A team in Oldenburg, in Germany, found their robins couldn't orient at all in wooden huts on the university campus. The cause turned out to be weak electrical noise from equipment around the city. Once the huts were lined with aluminium and grounded, the birds oriented perfectly again.
9. [4:51] **Rebecca Sutherland:** OK. Those three compasses get a bird into the right general area. But near the end of a journey, many birds switch to what they can see. They pick up familiar landmarks, things like coastlines, rivers and mountain ranges, much as you would on a road trip down the South Island.
10. [5:09] **Rebecca Sutherland:** Homing pigeons give us good evidence for this. Researchers at Oxford fitted pigeons with GPS trackers and found they flew along the same routes again and again. I'd expected them to follow railway lines, which are straight and easy to see, but in fact the birds mostly followed major roads, even turning at junctions.
11. [5:29] **Rebecca Sutherland:** Finally, smell, which was the most controversial idea. In Italy in the 1970s, Floriano Papi worked with pigeons that had lost their sense of smell. Near their loft they were fine. But when they were released at unfamiliar sites, a long way off, most of them never made it home.
12. [5:49] **Rebecca Sutherland:** Plenty of scientists doubted this, partly because birds were thought to have a weak sense of smell. Then, in 2015, a study looked at Scopoli's shearwaters, which are seabirds. Some had their sense of smell blocked for a short time. Over open ocean they got lost, but they could still navigate when they were flying along the coast, where they could use what they saw.
13. [6:12] **Rebecca Sutherland:** So what's the overall picture? Birds don't rely on a single system. They combine several, and switch between them depending on the weather and where they are. That's why they're so reliable.
14. [6:25] **Rebecca Sutherland:** For next week, I'd like you to read about a newer problem. We used to worry mainly about noise from cities, but the bigger threat to night-flying birds seems to be light pollution, which can draw them off course. We'll look at what can be done about it. Thanks, everyone.

**Welcome to the university library.** You will hear a librarian talking to a group of new students about the services offered by their university library.

Speakers: Dana Whitmore (female, north_american, university librarian, xai ara)

### Transcript

0. [0:42] **Dana Whitmore:** Hi everyone, and welcome to Hartwell Library. My name's Dana Whitmore, and I'm one of the librarians here. Over the next twenty minutes or so, I'll tell you how borrowing works, where you can study, and what kind of help you can get. So, let's start with borrowing.
1. [0:57] **Dana Whitmore:** Your student card is also your library card, so there's nothing extra to sign up for. Now, a lot of you will have heard that undergraduates can take out ten items at a time. That was true until last year, but the limit's now fifteen. Graduate students can have twenty, but for you, it's fifteen.
2. [1:15] **Dana Whitmore:** Okay, what if you keep something too long? We actually got rid of daily fines a couple of years ago, so you won't be paying a few cents a day. What happens instead is that your account gets frozen, so you can't check out anything else until the late item comes back. We'll only send you a bill for the replacement cost if something's more than six weeks overdue.
3. [1:33] **Dana Whitmore:** Right, course reserves. These are books your professors have put aside because the whole class needs them, and they're kept behind the main service desk. Some of you might think you can only read them inside the building. That used to be the rule, but these days you can take them out for up to four hours at a time. And you don't need to request them online or anything; just ask at the desk.
4. [1:53] **Dana Whitmore:** Let's move on to online resources. We subscribe to thousands of journals and e-books, and you can get to all of them from home. A few years ago, you had to install special software on your laptop to do that. Now you just sign in through the library website with the same email and password you use for everything else on campus. There's no need to register with us separately.
   - heard (WER 0.028): Let's move on to online resources. We subscribe to thousands of journals and ebooks, and you can get to all of them from home. A few years ago, you had to install special software on your laptop to do that. Now you just sign in through the library website with the same email and password you use for everything else on campus. There's no need to register with us separately.
5. [2:13] **Dana Whitmore:** And if we don't have something, we can borrow it from another library for you. That's called an interlibrary loan, and it's free. Journal articles are quickest; they usually arrive by email in two or three days. Books take a bit longer, normally about a week, although something rare or very old can take up to a month.
6. [2:32] **Dana Whitmore:** We also run workshops all semester, on things like citing sources and searching databases. Some new students also rush to book one-on-one appointments with a librarian in their first week. But honestly, the first thing I'd ask you to do is the online orientation tutorial on our website. It only takes about twenty minutes, and it'll answer most of your questions before you even have them.
7. [3:26] **Dana Whitmore:** Okay, now let me tell you about some of the spaces and services you'll see as we walk around. First, the group study rooms on the third floor. You can reserve them on our website up to seven days in advance, and I'd recommend that, because they fill up fast. And there's a myth that they're only for final-year students. That's not true at all; anyone can book them.
8. [3:45] **Dana Whitmore:** Next, the silent reading room on the fourth floor. It's completely quiet, and to keep it that way, you're not allowed to bring bags inside. There are lockers just outside the door for that. Some people ask if it stays open all night during exams. It doesn't, I'm afraid; that's the twenty-four-hour lounge on the ground floor.
9. [4:03] **Dana Whitmore:** If your own laptop breaks down, don't panic. You can borrow one of ours from the service desk, using your student card. They're very popular, so the rule is they have to come back to the desk before we close that same evening. You can't keep them overnight.
10. [4:17] **Dana Whitmore:** Then there's the writing center, where tutors help you plan and improve your essays. If you've seen old maps of the building, they show it on the second floor. But over the summer it moved down to the basement, next to the computer lab, so that's where you'll find it now.
11. [4:31] **Dana Whitmore:** Printing. Printers are on every floor, and you pay with your student card. Each semester, the first hundred pages are free, so you don't pay anything until you've used those up. After that it's ten cents a page, sorry, eight cents, for black and white.
12. [4:45] **Dana Whitmore:** And finally, the subject librarians. Every department has one, and they're the people to talk to about research for your assignments. During the day you can drop by their offices on the first floor, but a lot of students have classes then, so they also offer video calls in the evenings, until about nine. You'll find the links on the library website.
13. [5:04] **Dana Whitmore:** Okay, that's the overview. Let's head upstairs, and I'll show you the third floor first.

## l9|p4: succeeded

**The bicycle and social change.** You will hear a social history lecturer giving a talk about the development of the bicycle and its effects on society.

Speakers: Andrew Kerrigan (male, australian, university lecturer in social history, elevenlabs WLKp2jV6nrS8aMkPPDRO)

### Transcript

0. [0:43] **Andrew Kerrigan:** Good morning, everyone. Today I want to talk about something most of you probably rode as kids without a second thought: the bicycle. I'll start by tracing how its design developed during the nineteenth century. Then, since this is a social history course, we'll look at what it did to people's working lives, to women's freedom, and to the shape of our cities.
1. [1:04] **Andrew Kerrigan:** So, let's start in Germany in 1817. A man called Karl Drais built what's usually considered the first two-wheeled machine. It had no pedals at all. You sat on it and pushed yourself along with your feet. Now, why did he bother? One popular theory is that after a run of bad harvests, oats became terribly expensive, and lots of horses died or were sold off. So Drais was looking for a way to get around without relying on one.
   - heard (WER 0.025): So, let's start in Germany in 1817. A man called Karl Dreis built what's usually considered the first two-wheeled machine. It had no pedals at all. You sat on it and pushed yourself along with your feet. Now, why did he bother? One popular theory is that after a run of bad harvests, oats became terribly expensive, and lots of horses died or were sold off. So Dreis was looking for a way to get around without relying on one.
2. [1:33] **Andrew Kerrigan:** The next big step came in France in the 1860s, when pedals were fixed directly to the front wheel. These machines were officially called velocipedes, but people gave them a much less flattering name. They had wooden wheels with iron rims, and the streets were often cobbled, so in English they became known as boneshakers. I think you can imagine why.
3. [1:56] **Andrew Kerrigan:** Then, in the 1870s, came the penny-farthing, with that enormous front wheel and tiny back one. People often assume the big wheel was there for comfort, but that wasn't really the point. Because the pedals turned the front wheel directly, a bigger wheel meant you travelled further with each turn of the pedals. In other words, more speed. The downside was that a fall from that height could be serious, so the riders were mostly young, athletic men.
   - heard (WER 0.013): Then, in the 1870s, came the penny farthing, with that enormous front wheel and tiny back one. People often assumed the big wheel was there for comfort, but that wasn't really the point. Because the pedals turned the front wheel directly, a bigger wheel meant you travelled further with each turn of the pedals. In other words, more speed. The downside was that a fall from that height could be serious, so the riders were mostly young athletic men.
4. [2:24] **Andrew Kerrigan:** The real turning point was the so-called safety bicycle, which appeared in England in 1885. Its two wheels were roughly the same size, and the rider sat much lower. But the crucial change wasn't the frame. It was that the pedals now drove the back wheel by means of a chain, so you could get good speed without a giant wheel.
5. [2:45] **Andrew Kerrigan:** A few years later, in 1887, sorry, 1888, a Scottish vet living in Belfast, John Dunlop, developed the air-filled tyre. He didn't originally have bicycles in mind, actually. He was trying to give his young son a smoother ride on his tricycle. Within a decade, though, these pneumatic tyres were standard on bicycles everywhere.
   - heard (WER 0.036): A few years later, in 1888, a Scottish vet living in Belfast, John Dunlop, developed the air-filled tyre. He didn't originally have bicycles in mind, actually – he was trying to give his young son a smoother ride on his tricycle. Within a decade, though, these pneumatic tyres were standard on bicycles everywhere.
6. [3:08] **Andrew Kerrigan:** Right. So by the 1890s you had a machine that was safe, comfortable and, as factories began mass-producing them, increasingly affordable. That's when the great bicycle boom happened, and Australia was very much part of it. Let's turn to work. In the outback, shearers began riding bicycles between sheep stations, sometimes covering hundreds of kilometres. Before that, they'd have walked, or gone on horseback if they could afford a horse.
7. [3:36] **Andrew Kerrigan:** And on the Western Australian goldfields, around Coolgardie, cyclists were paid to carry letters between the mining camps and the nearest telegraph office. People sometimes assume they carried gold, but that would've been far too risky on a bike in the middle of the desert. Letters, on the other hand, were light, and they were urgent.
   - heard (WER 0.036): And on the Western Australian goldfields, around Coolgardie, cyclists were paid to carry letters between the mining camps and the nearest telegraph office. People sometimes assume they carried gold, but that would have been far too risky on a bike in the middle of the desert. Letters, on the other hand, were light and they were urgent.
8. [3:56] **Andrew Kerrigan:** Now, the bicycle probably had its most dramatic effect on women's lives. To ride comfortably, many women gave up the long, heavy skirts of the time. Some wore what were called bloomers, that's B-L-O-O-M-E-R-S, which were loose trousers gathered at the knee. It was a very visible challenge to ideas about how a respectable woman should dress.
   - voice got: Now, the bicycle probably had its most dramatic effect on women's lives. To ride comfortably, many women gave up the long, heavy skirts of the time. Some wore what were called bloomers, that's B, L, O, O, M, E, R, S, which were loose trousers gathered at the knee. It was a very visible challenge to ideas about how a respectable woman should dress.
9. [4:24] **Andrew Kerrigan:** But it wasn't only about clothing. A young, unmarried woman was generally expected to have a chaperone with her whenever she went out, usually an older woman from the family who'd keep an eye on her. On a bicycle, she could simply ride off on her own, and a chaperone on foot couldn't possibly keep up. So women gained a kind of independence they'd never had before.
10. [4:46] **Andrew Kerrigan:** Not everyone approved, of course. Some church leaders complained that cycling kept people away from Sunday services, but that applied to men as much as women. The fiercest criticism of women riding came from doctors, who warned it could damage their health. Some even described a condition called bicycle face, a strained, exhausted expression. Sounds ridiculous now, but it appeared in serious medical journals.
11. [5:13] **Andrew Kerrigan:** Finally, cities. We tend to think it was the motor car that transformed our roads, but cyclists actually got there first. In the 1890s, cycling clubs in Melbourne and Sydney, and in Britain and America too, lobbied governments to build smoother, better-maintained roads. The clubs also published maps for their members, but those they produced themselves. So cars later benefited from improvements that cyclists had campaigned for.
12. [5:41] **Andrew Kerrigan:** The other big change was where people lived. Before the bicycle, most working people had to live within walking distance of their jobs, so they crowded into the inner city. With a cheap bike, a clerk or a factory worker could live several kilometres away, out in the suburbs, where housing was cheaper and less crowded. So the bicycle helped start the outward spread of our cities, alongside the early trams, and well before the car.
13. [6:08] **Andrew Kerrigan:** Okay, that's the overview. Next week, we'll look at how the car pushed the bicycle aside for much of the twentieth century, and why it's now making a comeback.
   - heard (WER 0.034): OK, that's the overview. Next week, we'll look at how the car pushed the bicycle aside for much of the 20th century and why it's now making a comeback.

1. [0:58] **Jacinta Rowley:** Right, let's start at the main entrance at the southern end and imagine we're walking north. The main car park's on your left and the surf club's on your right. 3. [1:38] **Jacinta Rowley:** Okay, at the junction the coastal path turns right and heads east, while the bush track goes straight on north. The little hut opposite them, on the right, is the old rangers' hut, and that's locked now.
4. [2:01] **Jacinta Rowley:** Back on the coastal path, then, walking east. You'll pass the playground on your right-hand side. 5. [2:17] **Jacinta Rowley:** Keep going past the playground, and on the same side, right at the corner where the path turns north, you'll find the first aid station. There's a defibrillator and a first aid kit, and surf club volunteers check it every week.
6. [2:30] **Jacinta Rowley:** From there the path follows the cliff top north to the headland. 7. [3:24] **Jacinta Rowley:** Right, now closures. The old section that ran along the cliff edge past the blowhole has been closed for good. We had the budget to repair it, but our engineers said a path there simply isn't safe.
8. [3:47] **Jacinta Rowley:** There's also a temporary closure. We're resurfacing the stretch between the surf club and the junction, so that'll be shut for two weeks in May. 9. [4:05] **Jacinta Rowley:** During those two weeks you can't walk from the car park to the rest of the path. And the bush track's no help, because you reach it from the same junction. 10. [4:27] **Jacinta Rowley:** Now, dogs, which have caused a bit of debate. Dogs are still welcome, but they must be on a lead on the coastal path at all times. 11. [4:43] **Jacinta Rowley:** As for safety, you'd probably expect me to talk about falls, but in fact most of our call-outs are for heat. ## l10|p4: succeeded

**Bioluminescence in the deep sea.** You will hear a marine biology lecturer giving a talk about deep-ocean animals that produce their own light.

Speakers: Imogen Fairclough (female, british, university lecturer in marine biology, elevenlabs FF59babHL8N8gfTgtBMT)

### Transcript

0. [0:43] **Imogen Fairclough:** Good morning, everyone. I'm Dr Imogen Fairclough, and today we're continuing our series on life in the deep ocean. The topic is bioluminescence, that's the production of light by living things. I'll look first at how animals actually make light, then at why they do it, and finally at how scientists go about studying it.
1. [1:04] **Imogen Fairclough:** Right. Below about two hundred metres, sunlight fades almost completely, and yet something like three quarters of the animals living down there can produce their own light. So how do they do it? The process depends on a chemical called luciferin.
2. [1:21] **Imogen Fairclough:** Now, a few species manufacture luciferin themselves, but a surprising number can't. Instead, they simply take it in through their diet, by eating smaller creatures that already contain it.
3. [1:34] **Imogen Fairclough:** Once the animal has the luciferin, it's stored in special light organs. You might expect these to be on the head or on the back, where they'd be easy to see. In fact, in most deep-sea fish they're arranged in rows along the belly, and we'll come back to why that is in a few minutes.
4. [1:50] **Imogen Fairclough:** When the animal wants to glow, a signal from its nerves triggers a reaction. The luciferin combines with an enzyme called luciferase. For a long time people assumed water was the third ingredient, but it's actually oxygen that's needed, and without it, no light is produced at all.
5. [2:08] **Imogen Fairclough:** What comes out of this reaction is energy, released as light. And here's the remarkable part: almost none of it is lost as heat. Compare that with an old-fashioned light bulb, which wastes most of its energy warming up the room. That's why biologists often call this cold light.
6. [2:27] **Imogen Fairclough:** The light organ itself is quite sophisticated. Behind the light there's usually a reflective layer that works rather like a mirror, sending the light outwards. And in front, many species have a lens, which focuses the light into a beam, a bit like the headlamp on a car.
7. [2:43] **Imogen Fairclough:** What about colour? Coastal species, jellyfish near the shore for example, often glow green. But in the deep sea the light is nearly always blue, because blue light travels furthest through seawater. Red light, on the other hand, disappears very quickly.
   - heard (WER 0.049): What about color? Coastal species—jellyfish near the shore, for example—often glow green. But in the deep sea, the light is nearly always blue, because blue light travels farthest through seawater. Red light, on the other hand, disappears very quickly.
8. [3:01] **Imogen Fairclough:** Finally, timing. Some animals glow steadily for hours, but most produce flashes, and a typical flash lasts less than a second. Short flashes save energy and make the animal much harder to locate.
9. [3:15] **Imogen Fairclough:** OK, so that's how. Now, why? The most common use is defence. Remember those lights along the belly?
10. [3:57] **Imogen Fairclough:** Predators in the deep often swim below their prey and look upwards, watching for anything against the faint light from above. By glowing at just the right brightness, a fish can make its shadow disappear. We call this counter-illumination.
11. [4:13] **Imogen Fairclough:** Light is also used for hunting. The anglerfish is the famous example, with its glowing lure that draws in curious prey. But personally, I find the dragonfish more interesting.
12. [4:25] **Imogen Fairclough:** Unlike almost everything else down there, it produces red light, which most deep-sea animals can't see. So it works like a hidden torch, letting the dragonfish spot its victims without them noticing.
13. [4:38] **Imogen Fairclough:** The third use is communication. Some small shrimps and fish flash in particular patterns, rather like a code. Researchers think the main purpose is to find a partner, although some patterns may also be warnings to rivals.
14. [4:53] **Imogen Fairclough:** Right, the last section: how do we study all this? For most of the twentieth century, scientists relied on animals brought up in nets. The problem was that these delicate creatures were usually badly damaged on the way up, and many stopped producing light completely.
15. [5:11] **Imogen Fairclough:** Nowadays, we use remotely operated vehicles, which are robots controlled from a ship. You might think they'd need powerful lights, but bright lights actually scare animals away, so researchers often switch them off. Instead, the vehicles are fitted with cameras so sensitive that they can pick up even the faintest glow.
16. [5:32] **Imogen Fairclough:** And this research has benefits beyond marine biology. A glowing protein first found in a jellyfish is now used in hospitals and laboratories all over the world. Attaching it to cells allows doctors to watch how cancer spreads through the body, which has helped in developing new treatments.
17. [5:50] **Imogen Fairclough:** So, for next week, please read chapter six of the textbook, which covers vision in deep-sea animals. Thank you.

### Questions

**flow_chart_completion** — Complete the flow chart below. Write ONE WORD ONLY for each answer. (How deep-sea animals produce light)

- **31** Many animals obtain luciferin through their ______ rather than making it. → **diet** (solvers diet / diet; controller diet)
  - evidence: "they simply take it in through their diet"
  - en: The lecturer says only a few species make luciferin themselves; many get it from their diet by eating creatures that contain it.
  - ru: Лектор говорит, что лишь немногие виды вырабатывают люциферин сами. Многие получают его с пищей, поедая существ, в организме которых он есть.
  - uk: Лекторка каже, що лише небагато видів виробляють люциферин самі. Багато хто отримує його з їжею, поїдаючи істот, в організмі яких він є.
- **32** Luciferin is stored in light organs, usually in rows along the fish's ______. → **belly** (solvers belly / belly; controller belly)
  - evidence: "in most deep-sea fish they're arranged in rows along the belly"
  - en: She says you might expect the organs on the head or back, but in most deep-sea fish they are along the belly.
  - ru: Она говорит, что эти органы можно было бы ожидать на голове или на спине, но у большинства глубоководных рыб они расположены вдоль брюха.
  - uk: Вона каже, що ці органи можна було б очікувати на голові чи на спині, але в більшості глибоководних риб вони розташовані вздовж черева.
- **33** A nerve signal makes luciferin react with luciferase and ______. → **oxygen** (solvers oxygen / oxygen; controller oxygen)
  - evidence: "it's actually oxygen that's needed"
  - en: People used to think water was the third ingredient, but the lecturer says it is really oxygen.
  - ru: Раньше считалось, что третий компонент — вода, но лектор говорит, что на самом деле это кислород ("oxygen").
  - uk: Раніше вважали, що третій компонент — вода, але лекторка каже, що насправді це кисень ("oxygen").
- **34** Energy is released as light, with almost none lost as ______. → **heat** (solvers heat / heat; controller heat)
  - evidence: "almost none of it is lost as heat"
  - en: Unlike an old light bulb, the reaction wastes almost no energy as heat, which is why it is called cold light.
  - ru: В отличие от старой лампочки накаливания, эта реакция почти не тратит энергию на тепло. Поэтому такой свет и называют холодным ("cold light").
  - uk: На відміну від старої лампи розжарювання, ця реакція майже не витрачає енергії на тепло. Тому таке світло й називають холодним ("cold light").
- **35** A ______ at the front of the organ concentrates the light into a beam. → **lens** (solvers lens / lens; controller lens)
  - evidence: "in front, many species have a lens, which focuses the light into a beam"
  - en: The mirror-like layer is behind the light; the part in front that focuses the light is a lens.
  - ru: Зеркальный слой находится позади источника света, а часть спереди, которая фокусирует свет, — это линза ("lens").
  - uk: Дзеркальний шар розташований позаду джерела світла, а частина спереду, що фокусує світло, — це лінза ("lens").
- **36** The light is usually ______, as this colour travels furthest in seawater. → **blue** (solvers blue / blue; controller blue)
  - evidence: "in the deep sea the light is nearly always blue"
  - en: Green light is common in coastal species, and red disappears quickly; deep-sea light is nearly always blue.
  - ru: Зелёный свет часто встречается у прибрежных видов, а красный быстро исчезает. На глубине же свет почти всегда синий ("blue").
  - uk: Зелене світло часто трапляється в прибережних видів, а червоне швидко зникає. На глибині ж світло майже завжди синє ("blue").

**note_completion** — Complete the notes below. Write ONE WORD ONLY for each answer. (Why animals produce light and how it is studied)

- **37** Defence: lights on the underside stop predators below from seeing the animal's ______ → **shadow** (solvers shadow / shadow; controller shadow)
  - evidence: "a fish can make its shadow disappear"
  - en: Predators look up from below, so by glowing at the right brightness a fish makes its shadow disappear.
  - ru: Хищники смотрят снизу вверх, поэтому рыба, светясь с нужной яркостью, делает свою тень невидимой.
  - uk: Хижаки дивляться знизу вгору, тому риба, світячись із потрібною яскравістю, робить свою тінь невидимою.
- **38** Hunting: the dragonfish's red light acts as a hidden ______ → **torch** (solvers torch / torch; controller torch)
  - evidence: "So it works like a hidden torch"
  - en: The anglerfish uses a lure, but the dragonfish's red light, which other animals can't see, works like a hidden torch.
  - ru: Удильщик использует приманку, а красный свет рыбы-дракона, которого другие животные не видят, работает как скрытый фонарик.
  - uk: Вудильник використовує приманку, а червоне світло риби-дракона, якого інші тварини не бачать, діє як прихований ліхтарик.
- **39** Communication: flashing patterns are mainly used to find a ______ → **partner** (solvers partner / partner; controller partner)
  - evidence: "the main purpose is to find a partner"
  - en: Some patterns may warn rivals, but researchers think the main purpose is finding a partner.
  - ru: Некоторые узоры, возможно, служат предупреждением для соперников, но исследователи считают, что их главная цель — поиск партнёра.
  - uk: Деякі візерунки, можливо, слугують попередженням для суперників, але дослідники вважають, що їхня головна мета — пошук партнера.
- **40** Early research: animals brought up in ______ were often badly damaged → **nets** (solvers nets / nets; controller nets)
  - evidence: "scientists relied on animals brought up in nets"
  - en: In the twentieth century animals were collected in nets and were usually damaged on the way up.
  - ru: В двадцатом веке животных ловили сетями, и при подъёме на поверхность они обычно получали повреждения.
  - uk: У двадцятому столітті тварин збирали сітками, і під час підйому на поверхню вони зазвичай зазнавали пошкоджень.
